Entropic Risk-Averse Generalized Momentum Methods
Bugra Can, Mert Gürbüzbalaban

Abstract
In the context of first-order algorithms subject to random gradient noise, we study the trade-offs between the convergence rate (which quantifies how fast the initial conditions are forgotten) and the “risk” of suboptimality, i.e. deviations from the expected suboptimality. We focus on a general class of momentum methods (GMM) which recover popular methods such as gradient descent (GD), accelerated gradient descent (AGD), and the heavy-ball (HB) method as special cases depending on the choice of GMM parameters. We use well-known risk measures “entropic risk" and “entropic value at risk" to quantify the risk of suboptimality. For strongly convex smooth minimization, we first obtain new convergence rate results for GMM with a unified theory that is also applicable to both AGD and HB, improving some of the existing results for HB. We then provide explicit bounds on the entropic risk and entropic value at risk of suboptimality at a given iterate which also provides direct bounds on the probability that the suboptimality exceeds a given threshold based on Chernoff’s inequality. Our results unveil fundamental trade-offs between the convergence rate and the risk of suboptimality. We then plug the entropic risk and convergence rate estimates we obtained in a computationally tractable optimization framework and propose entropic risk-averse GMM (RA-GMM) and entropic risk-averse AGD (RA-AGD) methods which can select the GMM parameters to systematically trade-off the entropic value at risk with the convergence rate. We show that RA-AGD and RA-GMM lead to improved performance on quadratic optimization and logistic regression problems compared to the standard choice of parameters. To our knowledge, our work is the first to resort to coherent risk measures to design the parameters of momentum methods in a systematic manner.



Keywords
momentum-based first-order methods
\cdot

accelerated gradient methods
\cdot

heavy-ball method
\cdot

strongly convex smooth minimization
\cdot

stochastic gradient methods
\cdot

entropic risk
\cdot

entropic value at risk
\cdot

1
Introduction

First-order methods based on gradient information are frequently used for solving large-scale convex optimization and machine learning problems due to their favorable scalability properties to large datasets and to high dimensions
[
1
]
. Classic analysis of first-order methods such as gradient descent or its accelerated versions based on momentum averaging for convex optimization assumes access to the
actual gradients of the objective and provides convergence rate results to a global optimum. However, in many applications, it is often the case that we do not have access to the gradients but rather we possess a noisy (stochastic) unbiased estimate of the gradient. Examples would include but are not limited to stochastic gradient methods that estimate the gradient from randomly sampled subset of data points
[
2
]
for large-scale optimization or machine learning problems or privacy-preserving first-order optimization algorithms that add independent identically distributed (i.i.d.) Gaussian (or Laplacian) noise to the gradients for preserving the privacy of user data
[
3
,
4
]
. In such applications, because the gradients are subject to persistent stochastic noise, the iterates can oscillate around the global optimum without converging to it. Consequently, suboptimality of the iterates could often fluctuate around a limiting non-zero mean value
[
5
,
6
]
or could even diverge
[
7
]
. Therefore, controlling the statistical properties of suboptimality
S_{k}
at a given iterate
k
, such as the deviations of the suboptimality from its mean value or the probability that the suboptimality
S_{k}
exceeds a certain given threshold
t
, becomes key performance metrics to consider in addition to the convergence rates traditionally considered in deterministic optimization
[
8
,
9
]
.

When gradients are exactly known without any noise, it is well-known that momentum methods such as Nesterov’s accelerated gradient descent (AGD) method and Polyak’s heavy-ball (HB) method can significantly improve the convergence rate in terms of its dependency to the condition number of the objective function
[
10
,
11
]
. In fact, AGD method achieves the lower bounds for strongly convex smooth objectives in terms of its dependence to the condition number
[
10
]
. However, in the presence of stochastic gradient noise, the situation is different. It has been empirically observed that momentum-based methods amplify the noise in the gradients
[
12
]
and are less robust to noise in the sense that they need more accurate gradient information than gradient descent (GD) to achieve the same expected accuracy with the standard choice of parameters
[
13
,
14
,
7
,
15
,
12
,
16
]
. In fact, for momentum-methods, it is known that there are fundamental trade-offs between the convergence rate and the asymptotic suboptimality
[
5
]
and the performance of momentum methods is quite sensitive to the choice of parameters. If we view the suboptimality
S_{k}
at step
k
of a first-order algorithm as a random variable, then the existing guarantees for AGD and HB provides bounds on the expected suboptimality
\mathbb{E}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[S_{k}]
for every
k
as a function of the stepsize and the momentum parameter (see e.g
[
5
,
17
,
18
,
19
]
) and provide conditions under which a limiting distribution exists for the iterates and the suboptimality
[
17
]
. However, the expectation of a random variable is a point estimate and contains limited information about the underlying distribution (in the sense that two random variables can have the same expectation but can have arbitrarily different standard deviations or can have very different tail behavior). Therefore, existing approaches do not allow to optimize the parameters of momentum methods to control the tail probabilities
\mathbb{P}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{S_{k}\geq t\}
for some given threshold level
t
or to control the “risk" of suboptimality, i.e. deviations from the expected suboptimality.

In this paper, our purpose is to investigate the trade-offs between the convergence rate and “risk" of suboptimality in designing the parameters of a first-order algorithm and devise a computationally tractable strategy to achieve a particular trade-off in a systematic manner. For this purpose, we focus on the following general class of generalized momentum methods ( GMM) for minimizing a strongly convex smooth function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, i.e. for solving the unconstrained optimization problem

\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x),

(1)

when noisy estimates of the gradients are available
1
1

1

These methods were also called modified
modified accelerated methods
in
[
20
]
; however, to emphasize that they generalize AGD, HB; we use the name GMM.
. The iterations consist of

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\beta)x_{k}-\beta x_{k-1}-\alpha\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k}),

(2a)

\displaystyle y_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\gamma)x_{k}-\gamma x_{k-1}

(2b)

starting from the initialization
x_{0},x_{-1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k})
is an unbiased stochastic estimate of the actual gradient
\nabla f(y_{k})
(see Sections
2
and
6
for further details). GMM has three parameters where
\alpha>0
is the stepsize and
\beta,\gamma\geq 0
are called momentum parameters. Depending on how the parameters are chosen, GMM recovers
AGD, GD, HB, triple momentum methods
[
21
,
22
]
2
2

2

The original version of TMM from
[
22
]
has four parameters in it but
[
21
]
considers the version with three parameters as in our setting.
and robust momentum methods
[
23
]
as special cases. To quantify the risk of suboptimality
S_{k}
; we use
entropic risk
which is a well-known convex risk measure that depends on the risk-aversion of the user (measured by the risk-averseness parameter
\theta>0
) through the exponential utility function
[
24
,
25
]
. Our choice of entropic risk measure is motivated by several facts. First, entropic risk of suboptimality
S_{k}
is relevant to optimization practice because through Chernoff inequality, it implies bounds on the tail probability
\mathbb{P}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{S_{k}\geq t\}
for any threshold
t
(as will be discussed further in Section
2.2
). Second, we found that it was relatively easier to compute explicit upper bounds for it as opposed to other risk measures such as conditional value at risk (CV@R) which often require estimating the quantiles numerically
[
25
]
.

We start with asking the following questions:
(i)
“What is the effect of parameters on the convergence rate and on “the entropic risk of suboptimality?",
(ii)
“Can we design the parameters of GMM to achieve particular trade-offs between the convergence rate and the entropic risk?". In this work, we provide answers to these questions in the context of strongly convex smooth minimization problems. As a high-level summary, our first contribution is to obtain explicit non-asymptotic characterizations of the entropic risk of suboptimality
S_{k}
(Proposition
2
, Proposition
3
, Proposition
6
) as well as to obtain new non-asymptotic convergence results for GMM as a function of the parameters (Lemma
3.1
, Theorem
2
). This allows us to get performance bounds depending on the desired level of risk-averseness measured by the parameter
\theta>0
. Our second contribution is that by optimizing our entropic risk bounds over the choice of the risk-averseness parameter
\theta
; we obtain bounds on the entropic value at risk (EV@R) of suboptimality (Theorem
1
, Theorem
3
, Theorem
5
) where EV@R is a coherent risk measure with desirable properties. In particular, EV@R of suboptimality
S_{k}
at a confidence level
\zeta\in(0,1)
provides the tightest threshold
t_{*}
(that can be obtained through the Chernoff inequality applied to suboptimality) such that
\mathbb{P}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{S_{k}\geq t_{*}\}\leq\zeta

[
25
]
. Therefore, our results provide tail bounds as a function of the GMM parameters. Furthermore, our results demonstrate that for constant choice of stepsize and momentum parameters, EV@R converges to an interval around zero (as the iterations grow) with a linear rate that we can explicitly characterize (Theorems
1
,
3
, and
5
). In addition, our results show that there are fundamental trade-offs between the convergence rate (which determines the decay of the bias due to intialization) and the risk of suboptimality measured in entropic risk and EV@R.
Our third contribution is that we insert our EV@R and convergence rate bounds into a computationally tractable optimization framework which allows us to choose the parameters to obtain systematic trade-offs between the convergence rate and EV@R in an (approximately) Pareto optimal fashion. Consequently, we can design the parameters of GMM to minimize the asymptotic EV@R of suboptimality subject to rate constraints. This allows us to control the tail probabilities
\mathbb{P}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\{S_{k}\geq t\}
as a function of the parameters, where we show that our proposed design methodology for the parameters leads to an improved performance in practice compared to previously considered standard choice of parameters for GMM (Section
5
). Having provided a high-level summary of our results, in the following few paragraphs, we expand upon the technical details of our contributions.

First, for illustrative purposes, we focus on the special case when the objective is a strongly convex quadratic. In this case, existing guarantees for deterministic GMM
[
26
]
are asymptotic where the asymptotic rate is given by the spectral radius
\rho(A_{Q})
of the transition matrix
A_{Q}
which is a quantity that depends on the Hessian
Q
of the objective. We obtain non-asymptotic convergence guarantees (Lemma
3.1
); this is achieved by a careful analysis of the Jordan decomposition of the (typically) non-symmetric matrix
A_{Q}
. Then, we characterize the set of parameters for which the entropic risk is finite explicitly when the gradient noise is an isotropic Gaussian. This set is decreasing in the risk-averseness parameter
\theta
, we show that as
\theta\to 0
it recovers the set of parameters that leads to global linear convergence which we can also explicitly characterize ( see equation (
23
) and Lemma
E.1
). We show that limit of the entropic risk of suboptimality at step
k
converges to a value (linearly with the convergence rate
\rho^{2}(A_{Q})
) which we call infinite-horizon entropic risk (Proposition
1
). This result shows that convergence rate and the entropic risk are both
relevant for designing the parameters of GMM subject to random gradient noise and provides explicit characterizations of the risk of suboptimality at any iterate. Using these explicit characterizations, we show that there are fundamental trade-offs between the rate and the infinite-horizon entropic risk. Furthermore, we can show that a stationary distribution exists. We also characterize the entropic value at risk at stationarity for a given confidence level
\zeta
.

Second, we consider general strongly convex smooth objectives (that are not necessarily quadratics). In this setting, for GMM methods subject to
random gradient noise
, the existing results on the bounds for expected suboptimality are only local (except for particular choice of parameters which correspond to the special cases of GD, HB and AGD)
[
26
]
, i.e. holds only if the initial point is sufficiently close to the optimum and the existing analyses do not have all the constants explicitly available.
3
3

3

More specifically, Gitman
et al.

[
26
, Thm. 3]
obtain asymptotic convergence rate results for quasi-hyperbolic momentum (QHM) methods for quadratic functions where the constants
\epsilon_{k}
in this theorem are not explicitly available. This result can be used to provide local convergence results (but not global) for strongly convex functions and provides local convergence results for GMM as well if QHM parameters are appropriately scaled.
In the noiseless case, to our knowledge, explicit convergence rate results are available for only robust momentum methods which use particular choice of parameters (where all the parameters are expressed as a particular fixed function of the convergence rate)
[
23
, Theorem 21]
and also for TMM which corresponds to a particular (unique) choice of the GMM parameters
[
22
,
23
]
. To our knowledge, rate estimates for more general GMM parameters can only be obtained numerically. More specifically,
[
20
, Lemma 5]
shows that if for any given
\rho^{2}
, if there exists a positive semi-definite matrix
\tilde{P}
satisfying a particular
3\times 3
matrix inequality (that depends on
\rho^{2}
) then GMM admits linear convergence with rate
\rho^{2}
. That being said, both the rate
\rho^{2}
and the matrix
\tilde{P}
are unfortunately not explicitly available for general choice of parameters but rather estimated numerically by solving this particular matrix inequality. Furthermore, existing results do not provide explicit suboptimality bounds at a given iterate
k
in the presence of gradient noise.
Another issue is that characterizing the trade-offs between the convergence rate and entropic risk explicitly and accurately as a function of the parameters would require developing new convergence rate guarantees for a wider choice of parameters than those available in the literature. In order to address these points, we identify a family of parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
that are parametrized by two free variables
(\vartheta,\psi)
. We provide explicit convergence rate results for GMM and performance bound for the noisy GMM iterations provided that
\vartheta
and
\psi
satisfy an inequality we precise. The proof is based on extending the deterministic suboptimality analysis of GMM from
[
20
]
to the noisy case and constructing an explicit choice of the Lyapunov matrix
\tilde{P}
that serves as a solution to the matrix inequality provided in
[
20
, Lemma 5]
. These allow us to obtain explicit convergence rate results for a wider choice of parameters (Theorem
2
) that covers AGD and HB as a special cases. In particular, our convergence rate results for HB scales like
1-\Theta(\sqrt{\alpha\mu)}
for
\alpha
small enough which is comparable to the convergence rate of AGD. When
\alpha
is sufficiently small, our result shows an accelerated behavior, i.e. square root dependency to
\alpha
, and improves existing results from
[
27
,
28
]

which obtained
1-\Theta(\alpha\mu)
rate for HB (see Remark
4
). We then obtain explicit bounds on the finite-horizon entropic risk and infinite-horizon entropic risk depending on the parameters (Proposition
3
). Optimizing these bounds over
\theta
, we obtain characterization of the EVAR of
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
at any
k
which highlights the trade-offs between the choice of parameters. These results allow us to obtain bounds on the tail probability
\mathbb{P}\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\geq t\}
as a function of
t
. Moreover, by inserting the explicit bounds on EV@R and convergence rate in a computationally tractable optimization framework, we are able to select the stepsize and momentum parameters of GMM to achieve a desired trade-off between the convergence rate and the entropic value at risk. Since EV@R provides a bound on the tail probabilities, our framework allows us to optimize a bound on the tail probabilities as well.

Finally, in Section
5
, we provide numerical experiments that illustrate our results and practical benefits of our framework on quadratic optimization and logistic regression problems. While for simplicity of the discussion, we assumed that the gradient noise is isotropic Gaussian until the end of Section
5
; we discuss in Section
6
and Remark
6
that similar results hold for more general noise with (sub-Gaussian) light tails and for more general choice of parameters.

1.1
Other Related Work

Michalowsky and Ebenbauer
[
29
]
formulated the design of deterministic gradient algorithms as a state feedback problem and used robust control techniques and linear matrix inequalities to study their properties. Design and analysis of deterministic first-order optimization algorithms building on integral quadratic constraints from robust control theory have been studied in
[
30
]
. More recently, there has been a growing literature which uses dynamical system representations and control-theoretic techniques to study the convergence and robustness of optimization algorithms including
[
31
,
32
,
33
,
17
]
. Design of the parameters of momentum-methods to achieve trade-offs in the bias and variance of the iterates or expected suboptimality have also been studied in
[
5
,
23
,
34
,
35
,
7
,
36
,
37
]
. However, all these research works do neither study the “risk" of suboptimality nor the tail probabilities associated to suboptimality. Furthermore, the performance guarantees apply mainly to AGD, HB (and sometimes to their proximal variants) but not they do not apply to GMM with the more general choice of parameters we study in this paper. Scoy
et al.

[
22
]
introduced the triple momentum method (TMM) and showed that it improves upon the convergence rate of AGD for deterministic optimization. Gannot
[
21
]
studied robustness of TMM and gradient descent when subject to deterministic relative gradient error. TMM corresponds to a particular choice of the stepsize and momentum parameters in GMM (see
[
22
,
21
]
). As opposed to these papers, we consider stochastic noise in the iterations and we consider an alternative general family of parameters for which we can develop convergence rate and risk estimates.

Lan
[
38
]
introduced the AC-SA method for stochastic composite optimization, where the objective function is given as the sum of general nonsmooth and smooth stochastic components. It is shown that the AC-SA algorithm is an optimal method for smooth, nonsmooth, and stochastic convex optimization. Ghadimi and Lan
[
39
,
40
]
studied AC-SA further for solving strongly convex composite optimization problems, developed tail bounds for it and showed that it can achieve a performance in expected suboptimality matching the lower complexity bounds in stochastic optimization. Another multi-stage AGD method with optimal complexity for expected suboptimality is developed in
[
18
]
. Here, we focus on a more general class of methods (GMM) than AGD and focus on the design of parameters to achieve systematic trade-offs between the risk of suboptimality and the convergence rate instead.

For HB methods subject to random noise, there are many references in the literature that are not possible to list all. Among these most related to our paper, other than the references we mentioned above, Can
et al.

[
17
]
develops tight accelerated convergence results for quadratic problems for optimization and Kuru
et al.

[
3
]
provides privacy guarantees for the user data. For quadratic objectives, Loizou
et al.

[
41
]
shows accelerated linear rate in expected iterates and also linear rate in expected function values. Gadat
et al.

[
19
]
describe some almost sure convergence results for non-convex coercive functions and derive non-asymptotic results for strongly convex and convex problems with decaying stepsize. Li and Orabona
[
42
]
studied adaptive SGD with momentum and showed the gradient norm is small with high-probability in the non-convex setting. Sebbouh
et al.

[
43
]
obtains almost sure convergence rates for stochastic GD and stochastic HB. Yang
et al.

[
44
]
provides a unified convergence theory for stochastic AGD and HB with constant stepsize under bounded gradient assumption. In our setting, due to strong convexity, bounded gradient assumption does not hold and by exploiting strong convexity, we can obtain stronger guarantees than the convex case handled in
[
44
]
and in the other references listed above when the stepsize is smaller than a threshold we will specify.

Notations and Preliminaries

Throughout the paper, we use the notations
I_{d}
and
0_{d}
for
d\times d
identity and zero matrices, respectively. We denote non-negative real numbers by
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and positive real numbers by
\mathbb{R}_{++}
. The Kronecker product of two matrices
A
and
B
is denoted by
A\otimes B
. The set
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of all
\mu
-strongly convex and
L
-smooth functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We assume
\mu\neq L
, otherwise the class
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is trivial. Notice the for all
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the solution of the optimization problem (
1
) is unique which we will denote by
x_{*}
.
We say
f(n)=\mathcal{O}(g(n))
if
f
is bounded above by
g
asymptotically, i.e. there exist constants
k_{0}>0
and
n_{0}\geq 0
such that
f(n)\leq k_{0}g(n)
for all
n>n_{0}
. For functions
f:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
g:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we say
f(\theta)=o(g(\theta))
as
\theta\to 0
if for any constant
c_{0}>0
, there exists
\theta_{0}>0
such that
f(\theta)\leq c_{0}g(\theta)
for all
\theta
with
\theta<\theta_{0}
. We say
f=\Theta(g(\theta))
as
\theta\to 0
if there exists positive constants
c_{0},c_{1},\theta_{0}>0
such that
c_{1}g(\theta)\leq f(\theta)\leq c_{0}g(\theta)
for all
\theta
with
\theta<\theta_{0}
. We denote the multivariate Gaussian distribution with mean
\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and covariance matrix
\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
as
\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
. For any real scalar
x>0
,
\log(x)
denotes the natural logarithm of
x
. For an eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}(Q)
of a
d\times d
matrix
Q
, we will sometimes drop the dependency to
Q
if it is clear from the context and use the notation
\lambda_{i}
interchangeably.

Organization

In Section
2.1
, we provide the dynamical system representation of GMM which simplifies the presentation of our proofs. In Section
2.2
, we introduce the notions of “entropic risk" and “entropic value at risk" and discuss their relevant properties.
Then, in Section
3
, we study the infinite-horizon entropic risk and EV@R of GMM on strongly convex quadratic objectives where we explicitly calculate the infinite-horizon risk and provide an upper bound on the EV@R when the noise is an isotropic Gaussian.
In Section
4
, we extend our analysis to strongly convex objectives beyond quadratics where we present convergence results for GMM subject to Gaussian gradient noise as well as an upper bound on the EV@R. Based on these results, we propose the “entropic risk-averse GMM" which design the GMM parameters in a systematic way to trade-off the convergence rate with EV@R.
In Section
5
, we provide numerical experiments that provide the efficiency of the proposed “entropic risk-averse GMM" methods. Finally, in Section
6
, we discuss how our Gaussian noise assumption can be relaxed to more general light-tailed (sub-Gaussian) noise structure.

2
Preliminaries and Background

In the following, we consider GMM iterations subject to gradient noise. We will sometimes be referring to the resulting iterations by “noisy GMM" in the rest of the paper. We first discuss how the iterations with constant stepsize and momentum parameters can be formulated as a discrete-time dynamical system which will be helpful to simplify the presentation of our proof techniques. Then, we introduce the concepts of
entropic risk
and
entropic value at risk
.

2.1
Dynamical system representation of noisy GMM

Dynamical system representation of momentum algorithms have been considered in the literature which allows us to use tools from control theory for analysis purposes
[
30
,
20
,
23
]
. In our case, noisy GMM iterations can be represented as the
discrete-time dynamical system

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}+B\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k}),\quad\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k}):={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\nabla f(y_{k})+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}},

(3a)

\displaystyle y_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k},

(3b)

starting from (deterministic) initialization
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k})
is the noisy gradient,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}=\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k})-\nabla f(y_{k})
is the gradient noise at step
k
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}=[x_{k}^{\top},x_{k-1}^{\top}]^{\top}
is the state vector (that concatenates the last two iterates at time
k
) and the system matrices
(A,B,C)
are given by

\displaystyle A=\tilde{A}\otimes I_{d},\quad B=\tilde{B}\otimes I_{d},\quad C=\tilde{C}\otimes I_{d},

(4)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{A}=\begin{bmatrix}(1+\beta)&-\beta\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},\;\;\tilde{B}=\begin{bmatrix}-\alpha\\
0\end{bmatrix},\;\;\tilde{C}=\begin{bmatrix}(1+\gamma)&-\gamma\end{bmatrix}.

Regarding the noise sequence
\{w_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
, we first consider a simplified setting where the gradient noise is i.i.d. Gaussian and
w_{k+1}
is independent from the filtration generated by the iterates
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}
of noisy GMM. Such noise type has been previously considered in the literature in a number of papers to study convergence rate and robustness to gradient noise for momentum methods
[
5
,
33
,
17
]
and in privacy-preserving optimization algorithms
[
45
,
46
]
; it will allow us to obtain stronger guarantees for the “entropic risk" of the suboptimality we will introduce in the next section. However, our results extend to more general noise that can be light-tailed (sub-Gaussian when conditioned on the iterates) in a straightforward manner which will be discussed in Section
6
.

Assumption 1
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the gradient noise
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}=\tilde{\nabla}f({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{k})-\nabla f({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y}_{k})
is distributed according to
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
for some
\sigma>0
. Furthermore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}
is independent from the filtration
\mathcal{F}_{k}
generated by
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}
.

We note that in the noiseless setting (when
w_{k}=0
for all
k
), GMM reduces to GD, AGD and HB methods for specific choice of the parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
. For example, setting
\beta=0=\gamma
corresponds to the GD algorithm which consists of the iterations

x_{k+1}=x_{k}-\alpha\nabla f(x_{k}),

whereas if we set
\gamma=0
; we recover the HB method with iterations.

x_{k+1}=(1+\beta)x_{k}-\beta x_{k-1}-\alpha\nabla f(x_{k}).

Similarly, in the special case
\beta=\gamma
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}=0
, we obtain AGD method with iterations

\displaystyle x_{k+1}

\displaystyle=(1+\beta)x_{k}-\beta x_{k-1}-\alpha\nabla f(y_{k}),

\displaystyle y_{k}

\displaystyle=(1+\beta)x_{k}-\beta x_{k-1}.

2.2
Entropic risk and entropic value at risk (EV@R)

Before introducing the notion of EV@R, we first provide the definition of the
finite-horizon entropic risk

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
of the random variable (suboptimality at step
k
)
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
:

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\right)}],

(5)

where the expectation is taken with respect to all the randomness encountered (gradient noise) in the iterations,
\theta>0
is called the
risk- averseness parameter
,
\sigma^{2}
is the variance of the noise in each coordinate (see Assumption
1
). Entropic risk defined in (
5
) of the random variable
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
is a measure of the deviations from the mean of suboptimality
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
. It is a fundamental risk measure in statistics and arises naturally if we want to provide a bound on the tail probability
\mathbb{P}\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\geq a\}
at a threshold level
a

[
25
,
47
]
. To see this, note that the Chernoff inequality applied to the non-negative random variable
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
yields the following inequality for any
\theta>0
and
{a}_{k}>0
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a}_{k}}\}\leq e^{-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a}_{k}}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))}\right].

(6)

If we define the variable
\zeta:=e^{-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a}_{k}}}\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\right)}]
which is equal to the right-hand side and write
{a}_{k}
as a function of
\zeta
and
\theta
, this inequality is equivalent to

\mathbb{P}\left\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a}_{k}}(\theta,\zeta)\right\}\leq\zeta,\quad\mbox{where}\quad{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{a}_{k}}(\theta,\zeta):=\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(\frac{1}{\zeta}).

(7)

Entropic risk takes deviations from the mean of
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
into account. In fact, when
\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
is finite for
\theta
around zero, if we apply a first-order Taylor expansion in
\theta
, we obtain

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))}]=\mathbb{E}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]+\frac{\theta}{4\sigma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})|^{2}]+o(\theta),

(8)

which implies that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
accounts for both the mean and the variance of the suboptimality
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
(see also
[
48
, Example 2.1]
). We observe that if the risk parameter
\theta
gets larger; the deviations from the mean of suboptimality are penalized more.

By taking the number of iterations (horizon)
k
to infinity, one can also consider the following limit

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta):=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim\sup}\;\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim\sup}\;\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\right)}\right],

(9)

which is called the (infinite-horizon)
entropic risk
. If the distribution of the iterates
x_{k}
converges to a stationary distribution
\pi
, then under some further assumptions
4
4

4

such as the uniform integrability of the family of random variables
\big\{e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\right)}\big\}_{k\geq 0}
.
, we can replace the limit superior and the expectation to obtain

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\right)}\right],

(10)

where
x_{\infty}
is a random variable whose distribution is the stationary distribution
\pi
.
Similar to the bounds (
7
) and (
6
) for the finite-horizon risk,
for any
\theta>0
,
{a}>0
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\geq{a}\}\leq e^{-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}{a}}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*}))}\right],

and, if we set
\zeta=e^{-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}a}\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\right)}]
and write
{a}
as a function of
\zeta
and
\theta
, this inequality is equivalent to

\mathbb{P}\left\{f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\geq a(\theta,\zeta)\right\}\leq\zeta,\quad\mbox{where}\quad a(\theta,\zeta):=\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(\frac{1}{\zeta}).

(11)

We can see from the inequality above that the accuracy of the algorithm (measured in terms of asymptotic suboptimality) under confidence level
\zeta
is related to the entropic risk
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
. As a matter of fact, the
entropic value at risk
of
f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})
with confidence level
\zeta
, which will be denoted by
EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]
, is defined as the smallest value of
a(\theta,\zeta)
over the positive choices of
\theta
; i.e.

\text{EV@R}_{1-\zeta}\left[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\right]:=\underset{\theta>0}{\inf}a(\theta,\zeta)=\underset{\theta>0}{\inf}\left\{\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(\frac{1}{\zeta})\right\}.

(12)

The relation between EV@R and the entropy can be seen through the lens of duality. More specifically, the dual representation of the EV@R of a random variable
X
with an associated probability measure
P
is given by

{\displaystyle{\text{EV@R}}_{1-\zeta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[X]}=\sup_{Q\in\Im_{P}}(E_{Q}(X))},

(13)

where
\Im_{P}
is a set of probability measures with
\Im_{P}=\{Q\ll P:D_{KL}(Q||P)\leq\ln(\frac{1}{\zeta})\}
and
{\displaystyle D_{KL}(Q||P):=\int{\frac{dQ}{dP}}}

\left(\ln{\frac{dQ}{dP}}\right)dP
is the relative entropy of
Q
with respect to
P
(a.k.a. the Kullback-Leibler (KL) distance between
Q
and
P
) and
Q\ll P
means that
Q
is absolutely continuous with respect to
P

[
25
]
. This representation and connection to relative entropy is the reasoning behind the name “entropic value at risk". Thus, from (
13
), EV@R can be interpreted as a robust version of the expectation, and it quantifies the worst-case expectation of a random variable when its underlying distribution is perturbed up to an error term of
\log(1/\zeta)
in the KL distance.

In addition to its connection to entropy, EV@R also defines a coherent risk measure which is commonly studied in the mathematical finance and statistics
[
47
,
49
,
50
]
. Here, coherency of a risk measure is a desirable property which means that it satisfies the natural monotonicity, sub-additivity, homogeneity, and translational invariance properties
[
51
,
52
]
. Some of the other alternative well-known risk measures can be less desirable as they violate some of these assumptions. For example,
Value at risk
(V@R) is an alternative risk measure but is not coherent as it does not respect the sub-additivity property
[
25
]
.
Conditional value at risk
(CV@R) is an alternative coherent risk measure which is commonly used in statistics. However, one can argue that a disadvantage of CV@R is that it is not strongly monotone
5
5

5

This means that even if the suboptimality improves over one iteration satisfying
f(x_{k+1})-f(x_{*})\leq f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
pointwise and there is a positive probability that the suboptimality is strictly improved (i.e.
\mathbb{P}\left(f(x_{k+1)}-f(x_{*})<f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\right)>0
), it is possible that CV@R of the suboptimality is not strictly improved at iteration
k+1
compared to iteration
k
(see
[
47
, Sec. 2]
).
; whereas EV@R, is a strongly monotone risk measure
[
47
]
. We finally note that
EV@R provides the tightest possible upper bound (obtained from the Chernoff inequality) for both V@R and CV@R
[
25
]
. Therefore, our results on entropic value at risk also provides immediate bounds on CV@R.

Despite entropic risk measures being frequently studied in statistics and mathematical finance; their use in the study and design of optimization algorithms is relatively an understudied subject. To our knowledge, our work is the first to use entropic risk to design the parameters of momentum-based optimization algorithms for achieving robustness to the gradient noise and improving their statistical properties. Our approach also yields novel tail bounds for the suboptimality associated to GMM iterations.

3
Main results for strongly convex quadratic objectives

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a quadratic function of the form

f(x)=\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}Qx+p^{\top}x+r,

(14)

where the eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}(Q)
of the symmetric matrix
Q
satisfy

0<\mu=\lambda_{1}(Q)\leq...\leq\lambda_{d}(Q)=L.

Noticing that the global minimum
x_{*}
satisfies
0=\nabla f(x_{*})=Qx_{*}+p
, we can write
f(x)=\frac{1}{2}(x-x_{*})^{\top}Q(x-x_{*})
and
\nabla f(x)=Q(x-x_{*})
. Therefore, for such quadratic function
f
, the dynamical system (
3
) becomes

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}=A_{Q}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*})+B{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}},

(15)

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}:=[x_{*}^{\top},x_{*}^{\top}]^{\top}
,
z_{0}=[x_{0}^{\top},x_{-1}^{\top}]^{\top}
is the initialization and

A_{Q}:=\begin{bmatrix}(1+\beta)I_{d}-\alpha(1+\gamma)Q&-(\beta I_{d}-\alpha\gamma Q)\\
I_{d}&0_{d}\end{bmatrix}.

(16)

We observe from (
15
) that the distance to the optimum
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}
follows a (stochastic) affine recursion governed by the matrix
A_{Q}
where the randomness comes from the gradient noise. Since the noise is assumed to be centered, the expected distance follows the linear recursion
\mathbb{E}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*})=A_{Q}\mathbb{E}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*})
. Then, from Gelfand’s formula
[
53
]
, it follows that

\|\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}]\|\leq(\rho^{k}+\varepsilon_{k})\|\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}]\|,

for some non-negative sequence
\{\varepsilon_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
with the property that
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
. However, such a result is asymptotic as the sequence
\varepsilon_{k}
is not explicitly provided. Such asymptotic results have been obtained in
[
26
]
for the class of hyperbolic momentum methods (HMM) which cover GMM (by proper scaling of HMM parameters). In the next result, we obtain a new non-asymptotic bound on the expected distance with explicit constants. The proof is deferred to the appendix, it is based on explicitly computing the Jordan decomposition of the
A_{Q}
as a function of the GMM parameters and showing that the Jordan blocks are of size 2 in the worst-case.

Lemma 3.1
.

Consider the noisy GMM iterates
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}
satisfying the recursion (
15
) for minimizing a quadratic function
f
of the form (
14
) where the gradient noise obeys Assumption
1
. Then, we have for any
k\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}\|

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq\max_{i=1,2,\dots,d}\begin{cases}\frac{|c_{i}^{2}+2d_{i}+2|}{\sqrt{|c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}|}}\rho_{i}^{k}\|z_{0}-z_{*}\|,&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
(\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{4}+2)\sqrt{2\rho_{i}^{2k}+k^{2}\rho_{i}^{2k-2}}\|z_{0}-z_{*}\|,&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C_{k}\rho(A_{Q})^{k-1}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}\|,

(17)

where
\rho(A_{Q})=\max_{i\in\{1,..,d\}}\{\rho_{i}\}
is the spectral radius of
A_{Q}
with

\rho_{i}:=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}|c_{i}|+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}},&\text{ if }c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\geq 0,\\
\sqrt{|d_{i}|},&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(18)

and

c_{i}:=(1+\beta)-\alpha(1+\gamma)\lambda_{i}(Q),\quad d_{i}=-(\beta-\alpha\gamma\lambda_{i}(Q)),

\lambda_{i}(Q)
are the eigenvalues of the Hessian matrix
Q
of
f
for
i=1,2,...,d
,

C_{k}^{(i)}:=\begin{cases}\frac{|c_{i}^{2}+2d_{i}+2|\rho_{i}}{\sqrt{|c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}|}},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
(\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{4}+2)\sqrt{2\rho_{i}^{2}+k^{2}},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0,\end{cases}

and
C_{k}:=\max_{i}C_{k}^{(i)}
.

Remark 1
.

We note that Lemma
3.1
leads to new non-asymptotic performance bounds for deterministic GMM (where the gradient noise
w_{k}=0
for every
k
) as well. For deterministic methods, the iterates
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}
follow the recursion
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}=A_{Q}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*})
and the proof technique of Lemma
3.1
is still applicable. In this case, we can simply ignore the expectation in (
17
) and the deterministic GMM iterates
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}
will satisfy the bound
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|}\leq C_{k}\rho(A_{Q})^{k-1}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}\|
.

For
\rho(A_{Q})<1
, the expected iterates converge, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}]\to{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}
, and
\rho(A_{Q})
determines the rate of convergence where
\rho(A_{Q})
depends on the choice of parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
(see (
17
)). Next, we introduce the
stable set

\mathcal{S}_{q}
as the set of parameters for which the iterates converge globally in expectation, i.e.

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}_{q}:=\left\{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)~\mid~\rho(A_{Q})<1\right\},

(19)

where the subscript
q
in the definition of
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}_{q}
is to emphasize the fact that the objective
f
in question is a quadratic function.
In the next result, we show that the finite-horizon entropic risk measure,
r_{k,\sigma^{2}}
, converges linearly to the infinite-horizon risk measure
r_{\sigma^{2}}
for strongly convex quadratic objectives, where the rate of convergence is also governed by
\rho(A_{Q})
. These results show that convergence rate and the entropic risk are both relevant for designing the parameters of noisy GMM. Basically, the infinite-horizon entropic risk characterizes the tail probabilities of suboptimality in an asymptotic fashion, whereas the convergence rate characterizes how fast this asymptotic region is attained.

Proposition 1
.

Consider the noisy GMM iterates
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}
satisfying the recursion (
15
) for minimizing a quadratic function
f
of the form (
14
) where the gradient noise obeys Assumption
1
. For a fixed risk parameter
\theta>0
, the risk measure
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
is finite if and only if the parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
belong to the
\theta
-feasible set
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
:

\mathcal{F}_{\theta}:=\bigg\{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{R}_{++}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}~\vline~\;\color[rgb]{0,0,0}|c_{i}|<|1-d_{i}|\mbox{ and }\theta<2u_{i}\mbox{ for }i=1,2,\dots,d\bigg\},

(20)

where

u_{i}=\frac{(1+d_{i})[(1-d_{i})^{2}-c_{i}^{2}]}{\lambda_{i}(Q)(1-d_{i})\alpha^{2}},

(21)

with
c_{i}
and
d_{i}
as in Lemma
3.1
. Moreover, in this case we have
\rho(A_{Q})<1
and the finite-horizon entropic risk ,
\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
, converges to the infinite-horizon entropic risk ,
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
, linearly at a rate
\rho(A_{Q})
satisfying

\displaystyle|\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)-\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)|\leq\mathcal{O}\left(C_{k}^{2}\rho(A_{Q})^{2(k-1)}+C_{k}^{4}\rho(A_{Q})^{4(k-1)}\right),

(22)

where
C_{k}
is as in Lemma
3.1
, and
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
hides constants that depends on the initialization
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}
.

Proof.

The proof is deferred to Appendix
A.2
.
∎

By definition, the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
are increasing as
\theta
gets smaller, in the sense that
\mathcal{F}_{\theta_{1}}\subset\mathcal{F}_{\theta_{2}}
for
\theta_{1}>\theta_{2}
. Roughly speaking, this says that the less risk-averse we are (for smaller
\theta
); we have more flexibility in the choice of the parameters. To illustrate this point, in Figure
1
, we present the
\mathcal{S}_{q}
and the sets
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
for different values of
\theta
for the two-dimensional quadratic function
f(x_{(1)},x_{(2)})=x_{(1)}^{2}+0.1x_{(2)}^{2}
where
x_{(1)},x_{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}
and we take
\sigma^{2}=1
. It can be seen that as the risk parameter
\theta
increases, the set
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
gets smaller; i.e. we need to choose stepsize and momentum more conservatively to be able to limit deviations from the mean and control the risk.
6
6

6

In Figure
1
, we first plot the set
\mathcal{S}_{q}
in blue, and then the sets
\mathcal{F}_{0.25},\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\mathcal{F}_{2}
in purple, brown and green color respectively. If an area is colored multiple times, the plot will display the last color used. Consequently, the set
\mathcal{F}_{1}
is given by the union of the green and brown segment,
\mathcal{F}_{2}
is the union of green, brown and purple segment, and so on.
In fact, in Lemma
E
of the Appendix, we also show that
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
is a subset of
\mathcal{S}_{q}
for every
\theta>0
and we have

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lim_{\theta\to 0}\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\mathcal{S}_{q}=\left\{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathbb{R}_{++}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}~|~|c_{i}|<|1-d_{i}|,\;\;0<u_{i}\;\text{for $i$=1,...,d}\right\}.

(23)

This formula characterizes the set
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}_{q}
as a function of the GMM parameters and is new to our knowledge.

Figure 1:
The feasible region
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
versus the stable set
\mathcal{S}_{q}
for
f(x_{(1)},x_{(2)})=x_{(1)}^{2}+0.1x_{(2)}^{2}
where
x_{(1)},x_{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma^{2}=1
.

Next, we study the infinite-horizon entropic risk
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
and the entropic value at risk of noisy GMM on a strongly convex quadratic objective under Assumption
1
.
Let
U\Lambda U^{\top}
be the eigenvalue decomposition of
Q
where
\Lambda
is a diagonal matrix containing the eigenvalues. With the change of variable
\tilde{x}_{k}:=U^{\top}x_{k}
and
\tilde{w}_{k+1}:=U^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}
, we can see that
\tilde{x}_{k}
follows the recursion:

\displaystyle\tilde{x}_{k+1}=(1+\beta)\tilde{x}_{k}-\beta\tilde{x}_{k-1}-\alpha\Lambda\left((1+\gamma)\tilde{x}_{k}-\gamma\tilde{x}_{k-1}\right)+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}_{k+1}}.

Introducing
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}:=[\tilde{x}_{k}^{\top},\tilde{x}_{k-1}^{\top}]^{\top}
and
\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{*}:=[\tilde{x}_{*}^{\top},\tilde{x}_{*}^{\top}]^{\top}
, then the recursion becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k+1}-\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{*}=A_{\Lambda}(\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}-\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{*})+B{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}_{k+1}},

(24)

where with slight abuse of notation
A_{\Lambda}
refers to the matrix
A_{Q}
defined in (
16
) when we plug in
Q=\Lambda
. With a further change of variable
\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}:=\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}-\tilde{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{*}
, and using the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{w}_{k+1}}
have the same distribution we obtain

\displaystyle\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=A_{\Lambda}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}+B{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}},

(25)

here equality means that both sides have the same distribution. If
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
, then it follows from Proposition
1
that the finite-horizon risk measure will converge to the infinite-horizon risk and we have

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))}]=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\|S\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}\|^{2}}],

(26)

where in the last equality we used the eigenvalue decomposition of
Q
, i.e. we choose the matrix
S=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}[\Lambda^{1/2},0_{d}]
so that

\displaystyle f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})=\frac{1}{2}(x_{k}-x_{*})Q(x_{k}-x_{*})=\frac{1}{2}\|\Lambda^{1/2}U^{\top}(x_{k}-x_{*})\|^{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|S\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}\|^{2}}.

Next, we explicitly compute the entropic risk for noisy GMM under the isotropic Gaussian noise (Assumption
1
) and provide an upper bound on the EV@R of the asymptotic suboptimality. Since the noise has mean zero, we see from (
25
) that
\mathbb{E}[\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}|\mathcal{F}_{k-1}]=A_{\Lambda}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k-1}
where
\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
is the natural filtration generated by the iterates
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k-1}
and the matrix
V_{k}:=\mathbb{E}[\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}^{\top}]
satisfies the recursion

\displaystyle V_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\left(A_{\Lambda}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}+B{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\right)\left(A_{\Lambda}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}+B{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\right)^{\top}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[A_{\Lambda}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}^{\top}A^{\top}_{\Lambda}+A_{\Lambda}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}})^{\top}B^{\top}+B{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}^{\top}A^{\top}_{\Lambda}+B({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}})({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}})^{\top}B^{\top}]

\displaystyle=A_{\Lambda}V_{k}A^{\top}_{\Lambda}+\sigma^{2}BB^{\top}.

Given that
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
, we have
\rho(A_{\Lambda})=\rho(A_{Q})<1
and
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}]={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}
from Lemma
3.1
. Since
\rho(A_{\Lambda})<1
, it follows from the recursion above that the limit
V_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}V_{k}
exists (see
[
54
, Theorem 5 D.6]
) and satisfies the Lyapunov equation

V_{\infty}=A_{\Lambda}V_{\infty}A_{\Lambda}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}BB^{\top}.

(27)

Under Assumption
1
, we also observe from equality (
25
) that
\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}
is a centered Gaussian random variable for every
k
as
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}
is Gaussian. This implies that the limit
\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{\infty}=\lim_{k}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}
is also a Gaussian with a zero mean and with a covariance matrix
V_{\infty}
. Therefore, the distribution of the iterates
x_{k}
converges to a Gaussian distribution as well; let
x_{\infty}
denote a random variable with this limiting distribution. We also observe from (
26
) that
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2}X_{\infty}^{\top}X_{\infty}}]
where
X_{\infty}=\frac{S\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{\infty}}{\sigma}
has a Gaussian distribution. By computing this integral explicitly, in the next result, we obtain an explicit characterization of the infinite-horizon entropic risk. The details of the proof is given in Appendix
A.3
.

Proposition 2
.

Consider the noisy GMM iterates
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}
satisfying the recursion (
15
) for minimizing a quadratic function
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the form (
14
) where the Hessian matrix
Q
has the eigenvalues
0<\mu=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda_{1}(Q)\leq\dots\leq

\lambda_{d}(Q)=L
. Assume that the gradient noise obeys Assumption
1
.
The infinite-horizon entropic risk measure,
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
, is finite if and only if
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
, where
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
is as in (
20
), and is given as

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log\left(1-\frac{\theta}{2u_{i}}\right).}

(28)

Figure 2:
Illustration of the results of Proposition
2
and Theorem
1
for noisy GMM and noisy GD on
f(x_{(1)},x_{(2)})=x_{(1)}^{2}+0.1x_{(2)}^{2}
with
\sigma^{2}=1
.
Left:
The convergence rate vs. optimal (smallest) infinite-horizon risk attainable at this convergence rate for
\theta\in\{0.01,1,2\}
,
Right:
The comparison of
EV@R_{1-\zeta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]}
(straight lines) and its approximation
\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
(dashed lines) at confidence levels
\zeta\in\{0.95,0.5\}
.

On the left panel of Figure
2
, we illustrate the trade-offs between the infinite-horizon entropic risk measure and the convergence rate
\rho(A_{Q})
where we compare noisy GMM with noisy gradient descent when the objective is the quadratic function
f(x_{(1)},x_{(2)})=x_{(1)}^{2}+0.1x_{(2)}^{2}
where
x_{(1)},x_{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}
. More specifically, for GMM, we grid the parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
to find the best (smallest) entropic risk that can be attained at a particular convergence rate
\rho(A_{Q})
; i.e. among all the parameter choices
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
that leads to a particular rate
\rho(A_{Q})
, we calculate the smallest entropic risk that can be achieved. For noisy gradient descent, we follow the same procedure except that we take
\beta=\gamma=0
and only vary the stepsize
\alpha
. We repeat this procedure for
\theta\in\{0.01,1,2\}
.
We observe that faster convergence (smaller values of
\rho(A_{Q})
) is associated with worse (larger) entropic risk. Noisy GMM achieves better (smaller) entropic risk compared to noisy GD at any given convergence rate as expected; this is because GMM has more parameters than gradient descent to optimize over. We also observe that when the risk parameter
\theta
is increased, the best risk that can be achieved at a given convergence rate level will increase. In fact,
it can be seen from (
28
) that
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
is a convex function of
\theta
for
\theta\in(0,2\min_{i}u_{i})
and
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)\to\infty
as
\theta\to 2\min_{i}u_{i}
. Therefore, based on the formula (
12
),
EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]
can be computed easily up to high accuracy by minimizing a one-dimensional convex objective (in
\theta
) over the interval
(0,2\min_{i}u_{i})
. We use the entropic risk formula from Proposition
2
to provide
an explicit upper bound on the EV@R of suboptimality. This is achieved by approximating the optimal solution of the optimization problem
(
12
) that needs to be solved for computing the EV@R. The proof can be found in Appendix
A.4
.

Theorem 1
.

In the setting of Proposition
2
, consider noisy GMM with parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
.
The entropic value at risk at a given confidence level
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\zeta\in(0,1)}
of noisy GMM iterations at stationarity satisfies

\displaystyle\text{EV@R}_{1-\zeta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]}\leq\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma):=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2\theta_{0}\bar{u}}\left[-d\log\left(1-\theta_{0}\right)+2\log(1/\zeta)\right],

(29)

where
\theta_{0}=\frac{\log(1/\zeta)}{d}\left[\sqrt{1+2d/\log(1/\zeta)}-1\right]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}<1}
,
\bar{u}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\min}_{i\in\{1,..,d\}}\{u_{i}\}
and
x_{\infty}
is a random variable that is distributed according to the stationary distribution of the iterates
x_{k}
.

Example 1
.

(EV@R bounds for GD)
Theorem
1
implies
EV@R
bounds for noisy GD, if we set
\beta=0=\gamma
. In this case,
we have
u_{i}=\frac{1-(1-\alpha\lambda_{i}(Q))^{2}}{\lambda_{i}(Q)\alpha^{2}}
and
\rho(A_{Q})=\rho_{GD}(\alpha):=\max\{|1-\alpha\mu|,|1-\alpha L|\}
. The feasible set, on the other hand, becomes

\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\left\{\alpha\color[rgb]{0,0,0}>0~\vline~(1-\alpha\lambda_{i}(Q))^{2}<1-\frac{\theta\alpha^{2}\lambda_{i}(Q)}{2}\mbox{ for every }i=1,2,\dots,d.\right\}

Notice that as
\theta\rightarrow 0
this yields

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lim_{\theta\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{F}_{\theta}=\left\{\alpha~\vline~(1-\alpha\lambda_{i}(Q))^{2}<1\right\}=\left\{\alpha~|~\max\{|1-\alpha\mu|,|1-\alpha L|\}<1\right\}=\mathcal{S}_{q},

as expected from Lemma
E.1
. Moreover, for
\alpha\in(0,2/L)
, we can compute
\bar{u}
as

\bar{u}=\min_{i\in\{1,...,d\}}\{u_{i}\}\geq\frac{1}{L\alpha^{2}}\min_{i\in\{1,..,d\}}\{1-(1-\alpha\lambda_{i}(Q))^{2}\}=\frac{1-\rho_{GD}^{2}(\alpha)}{\alpha^{2}L}.

Hence, for
\alpha\in(0,2/L)
, the inequality (
29
) becomes

\displaystyle EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,0,0)=\frac{\sigma^{2}\alpha^{2}L}{2\theta_{0}(1-\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2})}\left[-d\log(1-\theta_{0})+2\log(1/\zeta)\right],

(30)

which relates the convergence rate to our EV@R bound.

As a direct consequence of the inequalities (
11
) and (
29
), and Theorem
1
, we have also the following corollary.

Corollary 1
.

In the setting of Theorem
1
, we have also

\mathbb{P}\big\{f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\geq\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\big\}\leq\zeta,

(31)

for any confidence level
\zeta\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(0,1)}
.

In order to illustrate that our EV@R bound
\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
provided in Theorem
1
is useful, we compare our EV@R bound to the actual
EV@R_{1-\zeta}(

f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*}))
value (which is defined as the optimal value of the minimization problem (
12
)) on the right panel of Figure
2
. Here, we consider
the objective
f(x_{(1)},x_{(2)})=x_{(1)}^{2}+0.1x_{(2)}^{2}
at
\zeta=0.95
and
\zeta=0.5
confidence levels. We grid the parameter space
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
and compute the
EV@R_{1-\zeta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]}
and the convergence rate
\rho(A_{Q})
corresponding to each choice of parameters
7
7

7

We find the
EV@R
at any given parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
by using grid search over
\theta\in(0,2\min_{i}u_{i})
.
on the right panel of Figure
2
, we
plot the best EV@R value achievable at a given rate
\rho(A_{Q})
with straight lines, we also plot the upper bound
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}^{q}
for the EV@R value in dashed lines. We observe that the upper bound
\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
is a good approximation in general to the actual EV@R value and the approximation quality is better when the convergence rate is closer to 1. For noisy GD, we follow the same procedure where we grid over the stepsize (and keep
\beta=\gamma=0
). These results display a trade-off between the convergence rate
\rho(A_{Q})
and
EV@R_{1-\zeta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]}
as well. This raises the natural question of what is the best
EV@R
achievable at a given rate level and this is achieved by which choice of parameters. The answer will naturally depend on the choice of the confidence interval
\zeta
and how close we want to be to the fastest convergence rate. Introducing
\rho(\alpha,\beta,\gamma):=\rho(A_{Q})
to emphasize the dependency of the spectral radius to parameters, the distance to the
optimum of GMM iterates decay at rate
\rho(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
in the absence of noise (Remark
1
); however the linear convergence rate in suboptimality will be
\rho^{2}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
as suboptimality is proportional to the squared distance to the optimum for strongly convex smooth functions. To our knowledge, the choice of parameters that optimizes the rate (minimizes
\rho(A_{Q})
) for quadratics is not explicitly known for GMM; however it is explicitly known for AGD. More specifically, the fastest rate for AGD is
\rho^{2}_{q,*}=(1-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3\kappa+1}})^{2}
where
\rho_{q,*}
is the smallest value of the spectral radius
\rho(A_{Q})
that can be attained by the AGD algorithm
[
30
]
. Given
\zeta\in(0,1)
, the following optimization problem looks for the best EV@R bound that can be attained if we would allow GMM to be slower than the fastest AGD rate by a certain percent:

\displaystyle(\alpha_{q},\beta_{q},\gamma_{q}):=\underset{(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}}{\text{argmin}}

\displaystyle\quad\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)

(32a)

s.t.

\displaystyle\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho^{2}}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{q,*}^{2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq}(1+\varepsilon),

(32b)

Here, the parameter
\varepsilon
can be viewed as a trade-off parameter that captures the percentage of rate we want to give away from the benchmark rate to achieve better entropic risk and tail bounds. Since we have an explicit characterization of the set
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}_{q}
,
\rho(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
and
\bar{E}^{q}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
as a function of the parameters, this three-dimensional optimization problem can be solved by a simple grid search on the parameters. In Section
5
, we will provide numerical experiments where we will illustrate how the parameter
\varepsilon
leads to systematic trade-offs between the convergence rate and the entropic risk as well as the tail behavior of the iterates.

4
Main results for strongly convex smooth objectives

This section extends our entropic risk and EV@R results from quadratic objectives to (more general) strongly convex objectives
f\in\mathcal{C}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. The convergence rate analysis of
deterministic
GMM (without gradient noise) can be carried out based on the Lyapunov function

{\mathcal{V}}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}):=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*})^{\top}P({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*})+f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}),

(33)

where the matrix
P
is symmetric positive semi-definite (PSD) with
P=\tilde{P}\otimes I_{d}
for a
3\times 3
matrix
\tilde{P}
. More specifically, in
[
20
, Lemma 5]
, the authors show that for any given
\rho^{2}>0
, if the matrix
\tilde{P}
satisfies a particular
3\times 3
matrix inequality (that depends on
\rho
) then GMM admits linear convergence with rate
\rho^{2}
. However, one limitation of existing theory for strongly convex minimization is that both the rate
\rho^{2}
and the matrix
\tilde{P}
are not explicitly available for general choice of parameters. By using an alternative Lyapunov function, convergence rates can be obtained for (deterministic) robust momentum methods which correspond to a specific parametrization of GMM (where all the parameters are expressed as a particular function of the convergence rate
\rho
) (see
[
23
, Theorem 21 and Table 1]
), however such results do not apply to GMM with more general choice of parameters. Furthermore, to our knowledge, existing results do not provide non-asymptotic suboptimality bounds for GMM at a given iterate
k
in the presence of gradient noise.

In the following result, we reparametrize the GMM parameters in two free variables
(\vartheta,\psi)
and obtain new (non-asymptotic) finite-time convergence rate results for GMM in this parametrization. The proof is based on constructing the Lyapunov matrix
P
in (
33
) explicitly where we take
P=\tilde{P}\otimes I_{d}
with

\displaystyle\tilde{P}=\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}}\\
-\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2}}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}}\\
-\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}}+\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2}}\end{bmatrix}^{\top},

(34)

which generalizes the Lyapunov functions previously considered in
[
20
,
5
]
for AGD. To our knowledge, these are the first non-asymptotic convergence results for GMM in the strongly convex case subject to noise. Previous results applied to the quadratics or gave only local convergence rates for non-quadratic strongly convex case (i.e. shows convergence only if initialization is close enough to the optimum)

[
26
]
. Furthermore, our choice of parameters has the advantage that it eliminates the need for solving the associated matrix inequality numerically to estimate the rate , and provides us a uniform analysis for AGD and HB methods.

Theorem 2
.

We introduce the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~\vartheta=1=\psi\},\;\;\;\;\mathcal{S}_{+}=\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~\psi>1\,\&\,1<\vartheta\leq 2-\frac{1}{\psi}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~0\leq\psi<1\,\&\,\max\left\{2-\frac{1}{\psi},\frac{1}{1+\kappa(1-\psi)}\right\}\leq\vartheta<1\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)\big|\psi\neq 1,\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{(1-\vartheta)\vartheta}{\kappa(1-\psi)}}\right]\left[1-\frac{(1-\vartheta)(\mu\psi^{2}-L(1-\psi)^{2})}{L(1-\psi)\vartheta}\right]\leq\left[1-\frac{(1-\vartheta)\psi}{\kappa(1-\psi)}\right]^{2}\right\},

with the convention that
\max\{2-\frac{1}{0},\frac{1}{1+\kappa}\}=\frac{1}{1+\kappa}
.
Under Assumption
1
, noisy GMM with the parameters,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\in\begin{cases}\{\frac{1-\vartheta}{L(1-\psi)}\},&\text{if }(\vartheta,\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{c}:=(\mathcal{S}_{-}\cup\mathcal{S}_{+})\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}_{1}},\\
(0,\frac{1}{L}],&\text{if }(\vartheta,\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{0},\end{cases}

(35a)

\displaystyle\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}=\frac{1-\sqrt{\vartheta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}}{1-\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\psi\mu}\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}{\vartheta}}\right],

(35b)

\displaystyle\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi}=\psi\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}.

(35c)

satisfies the inequality

\mathbb{E}[f(x_{k})]-f(x_{*})\leq\rho^{2k}_{\vartheta,\psi}\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0})+\left(\frac{\sqrt{\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}}(L\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}+\vartheta)}{2\sqrt{\vartheta\mu}}\right)d\sigma^{2},

(36)

where
\mathcal{V}_{P}
is the Lyapunov function given by
33
,
P=\tilde{P}\otimes I_{d}
with
\tilde{P}
as in (
34
) and the convergence rate is

\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}=1-\sqrt{\vartheta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\in(0,1)}.

Remark 2
.

(More general noise) The proof of Theorem
2
uses only the unbiasedness of the noise, i.e.
\mathbb{E}[w_{k+1}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0
, and the boundedness of the conditional second moments
\mathbb{E}[\|w_{k+1}\|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]
where
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is the natural filtration generated by the iterates
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}
. Therefore, as long as the noise sequence
\{w_{k+1}\}_{k\geq 0}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[w_{k+1}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0
and
\mathbb{E}[\|w_{k+1}\|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq\sigma^{2}d
for some
\sigma^{2}>0
and for every
k
, our results would still hold verbatim.

Remark 3
.

(AGD case) The case
(\vartheta,\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
(when
\psi=\vartheta=1
) in Theorem
2
yields the AGD algorithm with momentum parameter
\beta=\frac{1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu}}{1+\sqrt{\alpha\mu}}
which has been studied in the literature. In this case, our rate result recovers the existing rate results
\rho^{2}=1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu}
for the AGD
[
20
,
5
]
.

Remark 4
.

(HB case)

The set
\mathcal{S}_{-}
given in Theorem
2
allows
\psi=0
and it can be easily shown that
\{(\vartheta,0)|\vartheta\in[\frac{\kappa}{1+\kappa},1)\}
is contained in the stable set
\mathcal{S}_{c}
. Therefore, Theorem
2
recovers the Polyak’s heavy ball method (HB) with parameters
\alpha=\frac{1-\vartheta}{L}
and
\beta=\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta(1-\vartheta)}{\kappa}}\right]\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{(1-\vartheta)}{\kappa\vartheta}}\right]
, for
\vartheta\in[\frac{\kappa}{1+\kappa},1)
and implies that deterministic HB can achieve the linear convergence rate
\rho^{2}=1-\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta(1-\vartheta)}{\kappa}}
on strongly convex smooth objectives. By setting
\vartheta=1-\alpha L
, this implies that for
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{L(\kappa+1)}]
and
\beta=[1-\sqrt{(1-\alpha L)\alpha\mu}][1-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\mu}{1-\alpha L}}]
, HB admits the convergence rate
\rho^{2}=1-\sqrt{(1-\alpha L)\alpha\mu}
. Previously in
[
28
]
, it was shown that deterministic HB with parameters
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{L}]
,
\beta=\sqrt{(1-\alpha\mu)(1-\alpha L)}
can achieve the rate
\rho^{2}_{HB}=1-\alpha\mu
on strongly convex non-quadratic objectives. For
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{L(\kappa+1)}]
, to our knowledge convergence rate we prove for HB is faster than existing rate,
\rho^{2}_{HB}
, from the literature. Our rate for HB scales with
\sqrt{\alpha}
similar to the rate of AGD. In
[
27
]
, it is shown that HB method subject to noise assumption discussed in Remark
2
satisfies
\mathbb{E}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]\leq\mathcal{O}(r^{k}+\frac{L}{\mu}\alpha\sigma^{2}d)
for
r=\max(1-\alpha\mu,\beta)
provided that
k\geq k_{0}:=\lfloor\frac{\log 0.5}{\log(\beta)}\rfloor
. If the stepsize is small enough, or if the target accuracy
\varepsilon
(in expected suboptimality) is small enough, then our result leads to a better iteration complexity.

One natural question to ask is how large the other set
\mathcal{S}_{c}=(\mathcal{S}_{-}\cup\mathcal{S}_{+})\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}_{1}}
for which we can provide performance bounds is. In Lemma
B.2
of the Appendix, we show that the set
\mathcal{S}_{c}
is non-empty and contains non-trivial parameter choices in addition to the AGD and HB cases we discussed above. In Figure
3
, we also plot the range of parameters
\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}
and
\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}/\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi}
on an example when
(\vartheta,\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{c}
to illustrate our results.

Theorem
2
shows that noisy GMM converges to a neighborhood of the optimum. The suboptimality bound (
36
) is a sum of two terms: A “bias term" that decays exponentially with rate
\rho^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}
and is about how fast the suboptimality at the initialization decays and a “variance term" due to noise that characterizes the asymptotic suboptimality (and the radius of the neighborhood that the iterates belong to in expectation asymptotically). There are classic bias variance trade-offs in the bound we obtain in the sense that when the rate
\rho^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}
gets smaller, the bias term will get smaller but the variance term will get larger.
The inequality (
36
) provides performance bounds on the expected suboptimality but Theorem
2
lacks providing further information about the deviations from the expected suboptimality. In the next proposition, we obtain a characterization of the finite-horizon risk which captures information about the deviations from the expected suboptimality as well as the tail probabilities of the iterates. The proof is based on bounding the suboptimality
f(x_{k+1})-f(x_{*})
at step
k+1
in terms of a decay in the Lyapunov function
V_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})
at the previous step
k
and a random error term that depends on the noise
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}
and the difference
x_{k}-x_{k-1}
(see Lemma
B.3
from the Appendix) and then integrating the noise along the GMM iterations using the properties of the moment generating function of Gaussian distributions. We can then show that the finite-horizon entropic risk
\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
decays linearly with rate
\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}
to a limiting value (in the sense that
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
exists for which we can provide an upper bound). For any
\theta>0
, due to the random error term that needs to be exponentially integrated, the convergence rate
\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}
we obtain for the entropic risk is slower than the convergence rate
{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}
we have previously showed for the expected suboptimality in Theorem
2
, i.e. we have
{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}<\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}<1
. However, in the limit as
\theta\to 0
,
\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}\to{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}
; this is because as
\theta\to 0
,
\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)\to\mathbb{E}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]
(see (
8
)) in which case the entropic risk captures the expected suboptimality previously studied in Theorem
2
.

Proposition 3
.

Consider the noisy GMM iterations for
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where the parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
(as a function of
(\vartheta,\psi)
) are chosen as in Theorem
2
. Assume the gradient noise satisfies Assumption
1
.
Then, the finite-horizon entropic risk
\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
is finite for

\displaystyle\theta<\theta^{g}_{u}:=\frac{2\sqrt{\vartheta\mu}}{\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(8\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}\sqrt{\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}}+\sqrt{\vartheta\mu}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L))},

(37)

with

\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}=\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu}\left(2(\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}-\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi})^{2}+(1-\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)^{2}(1+2\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi}+2\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2})\right)+\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}}(1-\sqrt{\vartheta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}),

and admits the bound

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)<\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\big(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L\big)}{(1-\bar{\rho}^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi})(2-\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\big(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L\big))}+\bar{\rho}^{2k}_{\vartheta,\psi}2\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}),

(38)

where
\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}<1
is given by the formula

\bar{\rho}^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{\vartheta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}+\frac{\theta 4\alpha^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}}{2-\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}\right)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\Big(1-\sqrt{\vartheta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}+\frac{\theta 4\alpha^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}}{2-\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}\Big)^{2}+\frac{16\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}}{2-\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}}.

(39)

Consequently, taking limit superior of both sides of (
38
), we obtain

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\big(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L\big)}{(1-\bar{\rho}^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi})(2-\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\big(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L\big))}.

In the next result, by plugging the entropic risk bound into the EV@R formula (
12
) and approximating the solution of the optimization problem over
\theta
in (
12
), we obtain an upper bound for the EV@R of the suboptimality
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
for every
k
.

Theorem 3
.

Consider the noisy GMM to minimize the objective
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

under the setting of Proposition
3
. Let
\varphi\in(0,1)
be a fixed constant and let the confidence level
\zeta\in(0,1)
be given. Set
\theta_{\varphi}^{g}:=\varphi\theta_{u}^{g}
and

\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi}:=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{\vartheta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}+\frac{\theta_{\varphi}^{g}4\alpha^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}}{2-\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}\right)\\
+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\Big(1-\sqrt{\vartheta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}+\frac{\theta_{\varphi}^{g}4\alpha^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}}{2-\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}\Big)^{2}+\frac{16\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}}{2-\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}}.

(40)

If confidence level satisfies the condition

\log(\frac{1}{\zeta})\leq\frac{d}{2(1-\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi})}\left(\frac{\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}{2-\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}\right)^{2},

(41)

then, the EV@R of the
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
at iteration
k\geq 1
admits the bound

EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq}\frac{\sigma^{2}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{1-\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi}}}+\sqrt{2\log(1/\zeta)}\right)^{2}+(\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi})^{k}2\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}).

(42)

In the case (
41
) does not hold, we have

EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq}\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}{(1-\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi})(2-\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L))}+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta_{\varphi}^{g}}\log(1/\zeta)+(\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi})^{k}2\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}).

(43)

Moreover, the EV@R of the suboptimality satisfies asymptotically

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\limsup_{k\to\infty}}\;EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}k})-f(x_{*})]\\
\leq\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}:=\begin{cases}\frac{\sigma^{2}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{d}{1-\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi}}}+\sqrt{2\log(1/\zeta)}\right)^{2},&\text{ if \eqref{cond: conf-lev-gauss} holds.}\\
\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}{(1-\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi})(2-\theta_{\varphi}^{g}\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L))}+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta_{\varphi}^{g}}\log(1/\zeta),&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(44)

Let us denote
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}
by
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}(\vartheta,\psi)
for the bound provided in Theorem
3
to emphasize its dependency on the parameters
\vartheta
and
\psi
. A consequence of (
7
) and Theorem
3
is the following corollary which provides us information about the tail probabilities of suboptimality.

Corollary 2
.

Consider noisy GMM under the setting of Proposition
3
. Let risk parameter
\theta
satisfy
\theta<\theta_{u}^{g}
, then the tail probability of suboptimality admits the following bound,

\mathbb{P}\left\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\geq t_{\zeta}\right\}<e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\big(\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2k}2\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0})-t_{\zeta}\big)+\frac{\theta d\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L)}{2(1-\bar{\rho}^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi})(2-\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}L))}},

(45)

where
z_{0}=[x_{0}^{\top},x_{-1}^{\top}]^{\top}
is the initialization and
\bar{\rho}^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}
is as given in Proposition
3
. Furthermore, for a given confidence level
\zeta\in(0,1)
and a constant
\varphi\in(0,1)
, optimizing over the choice of
\theta
,

\mathbb{P}\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\geq\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}(\vartheta,\psi)+(\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi})^{k}2\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0})\}\leq\zeta,

(46)

where
\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi}
as given in (
40
).

Remark 5
.

(More general noise)
In Proposition
3
and Theorem
3
, we assumed the gradient noise
w_{k}
to be additive, i.i.d. Gaussian with a covariance matrix
\sigma^{2}I_{d}
. Our results would hold for Gaussian noise with an arbitrary covariance matrix as well. More specifically, if the gradient noise
w_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{w_{k}})
is independent from the past iterates
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k-1}
and satisfies
\Sigma_{w_{k}}\preceq\sigma^{2}I_{d}
for every
k
, then our proof techniques extend and the same results would hold. In Section
6
, we will also obtain results for more general noise structure that is not necessarily additive allowing sub-Gaussian tails.

The parameter choices studied in Theorem
3
and Proposition
3
captures the AGD method (when
\vartheta=1=\psi
), HB method (when
\psi=0
) and GMM but requires the momentum term
\beta>0
. Therefore, these results do not directly apply to noisy gradient descent where
\beta=\gamma=0
. Using similar techniques we used to derive Proposition
3
,
in the following we obtain bounds on the entropic risk of suboptimality for noisy GD.

Proposition 4
.

Consider the noisy GD iterations
\{x_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
to minimize an objective
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with stepsize
\alpha\in(0,\frac{2}{\mu+L}]
where the gradient noise obeys Assumption
