1
. If the risk parameter
\theta>0
satisfies the condition

\theta<\frac{2(1-\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2})}{L\alpha^{2}},

(47)

where
\rho_{GD}(\alpha)=\max\{|1-\alpha\mu|,|1-\alpha L|\}
, then the finite-horizon entropic risk satisfies,

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)<\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha^{2}L}{2(1-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2})-\theta\alpha^{2}L}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\big(\bar{\rho}_{GD}(\alpha)\big)^{2k}\frac{L\|x_{0}-x_{*}\|^{2}}{2},

(48)

where
\bar{\rho}_{GD}^{2}:=\frac{\rho_{GD}(\alpha)}{1-\theta\alpha^{2}(L/2)}\in[0,1)
. Similarly, the infinite-horizon entropic risk admits the following bound:

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha^{2}L}{2(1-\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2})-\theta\alpha^{2}L}.

(49)

For
\alpha\in(0,\frac{2}{L+\mu})
, the upper bound
\bar{\theta}:=\frac{2(1-\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2})}{L\alpha^{2}}
on
\theta
in (
47
) is a decreasing function of
\alpha
whereas the rate
\rho_{GD}(\alpha)
is a decreasing function of
\alpha
. Therefore, for a given
\alpha_{0}\in(0,\frac{2}{L+\mu})
, if we are more risk-averse and consequently if we want to increase
\theta
beyond
\bar{\theta}_{0}=\frac{2(1-\rho_{GD}(\alpha_{0})^{2})}{L\alpha^{2}_{0}}
, then in Proposition
4
, our analysis applies only if we give up from the rate
\rho_{GD}(\alpha_{0})
by choosing a smaller stepsize than
\alpha_{0}
. This phenomenon is not a shortcoming of our analysis in the sense that for the case of quadratic objectives (which are also in the class
\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
) where we can do exact computations, we also see a similar behaviour.
In the next result, we obtain EV@R results for gradient descent based on the previous result and the formula (
12
).

Theorem 4
.

Consider the noisy GD under the setting of Proposition
4
. Let confidence level
\zeta\in(0,1)
be given. The entropic value at risk of noisy GD admits the bound,

EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}\alpha^{2}L}{2(1-\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2})}\left(\sqrt{2\log(1/\zeta)}+\sqrt{d}\right)^{2}+\Big(\frac{\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2}}{1-\varphi_{GD}(1-\rho_{GD}(\alpha)^{2})}\Big)^{k}\frac{L\|x_{0}-x_{*}\|^{2}}{2},

(50)

where
\varphi_{GD}=\frac{\sqrt{2\log(1/\zeta)}}{\sqrt{2\log(1/\zeta)}+\sqrt{d}}
, and
\alpha\leq\frac{2}{\mu+L}
.

Figure 3:
The EV@R bound
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}
(
Left
) and the rate
\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}/\rho_{*}^{2}
(
Right
) on stable set
\mathcal{S}_{c}
at
\varphi=0.99
and
\zeta=0.99
confidence level where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{10}
,
L=1
,
\mu=0.1
for the objective function and the Gaussian noise is
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{10})
.

In Figure
3
, on the left panel, we plot the EV@R bound
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}
as a function of the parameters allowed by Theorem
2
. More specifically, for visualization purposes, we consider the two dimensional set

\mathcal{A}:=\{(\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi},\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}/\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi}):({\vartheta,\psi})\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{S}_{c}\}

and compute and plot the EV@R for every point in
\mathcal{A}
based on Theorem
3
for confidence level
\zeta=0.99
. On the other hand, on the right panel of Figure
3
, we plot the relative convergence rate

\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}/\rho_{*}
for every point in the set
\mathcal{A}
where
\rho_{*}^{2}=1-\sqrt{\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\mu}
is the fastest convergence rate we can certify (corresponds to the choice
\vartheta=\psi=1
) in Theorem
2
. We take the noise variance
\sigma^{2}=1
,
d=10
, and we set
\varphi=0.99
. Similar to the quadratic case, our results show that faster convergence, i.e. smaller values of the rate, is associated with larger values of the upper bound on the risk
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}
. We also observe that decreasing the stepsize in this region leads to a deterioration in the convergence rate while improving the risk bound
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}
. These results highlight the trade-offs between the convergence rate and the entropic risk and tail probabilities at stationarity (as
k\to\infty)
.
Motivated by these results, we consider the following optimization problem to design the parameters of GMM algorithms to achieve systematic trade-offs between the relative convergence rate
\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}/\rho_{*}
(which will be controlled by the parameter
\varepsilon
below) and the entropic risk:

\displaystyle(\vartheta_{\star},\psi_{\star})=\underset{(\vartheta,\psi)\in{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathcal{S}_{c}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{0}}{\text{argmin}}

\displaystyle\;\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}(\vartheta,\psi)

(51a)

s.t.

\displaystyle\frac{\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}}{\rho_{*}^{2}}\leq(1+\varepsilon).

(51b)

The parameter
\varepsilon
is a trade-off parameter as before, it captures what percent of rate we want to give up from the fastest convergence rate
\rho_{*}^{2}
to ensure better entropic risk bounds.
Since the stepsize and momentum parameters are parametrized by
(\vartheta,\psi)
, once we find the optimal
(\vartheta_{\star},\psi_{\star})
; we can calculate the optimal
parameters directly based on the relationship (
35
). We call the GMM algorithm with this choice of parameters
entropic-risk averse GMM
. In the next section, we study the performance of noisy GMM algorithms numerically and illustrate our theoretical results.

5
Numerical Experiments

In the numerical experiments, we first consider the following quadratic optimization problem where the objective in (
1
) is given by

f(x)=\frac{1}{2}x^{\top}Qx+b^{\top}x+\frac{\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle\mbox{reg}}}{2}\|x\|^{2},

(52)

in dimension
d=10
with
b=\frac{1}{\|\tilde{b}\|}\tilde{b}
for
\tilde{b}=[1,...,1]\in\mathbb{R}^{10}
,
Q
is a diagonal matrix with diagonals
Q_{ii}=i^{2}
for
i\in\{1,..,10\}
, and
\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle\mbox{reg}}=5
is a regularization parameter. We find the parameters
(\alpha_{q},\beta_{q},\gamma_{q})
of the (entropic) risk-averse GMM (RA- GMM), by solving the optimization problem (
32
) using grid search over
\mathcal{S}_{q}
where we set the trade-off parameter as
\varepsilon=0.25
at a confidence level
\zeta=0.95
. We take gradient noise according to Assumption
1
with
\sigma^{2}=1
. For the (entropic) risk-averse AGD (RA-AGD), we consider the problem (
32
) but also enforce the additional constraint
\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}=\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi}
using the same
\varepsilon
and
\zeta
values. For comparison purposes, we also consider GD with standard parameters
(\alpha=\frac{1}{L},\beta=\gamma=0)
used in deterministic optimization, as well as AGD with standard parameters
(\alpha=1/L,\beta=\gamma=\frac{\sqrt{L}-\sqrt{\mu}}{\sqrt{L}+\sqrt{\mu}})
used in deterministic optimization
[
20
]
.

In Figure
4
, we compare RA- GMM, RA-AGD, AGD and GD algorithms.

Figure 4:
(Left panel)
The expected suboptimality versus iterations for GD, AGD, RA-AGD and RA- GMM.
(Right panel)
The cumulative distribution of the suboptimality of the last iterates for GD, AGD, RA-AGD and RA- GMM after
k=300
iterations.

We initialized all the algorithms at
x_{0}=[1,...,1]\in\mathbb{R}^{10}
. We generated 50 sample paths
\{f(x_{0}^{(i)}),...,f(x_{300}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{50}
by running the algorithms for 300 iterations, then computed the empirical mean and standard deviation of the suboptimality
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
based on the samples
f(x_{k}^{(i)})-f(x_{*})
at every iteration
k\leq 300
. The left panel of Figure
4
displays the average suboptimality,
(\bar{f}_{1},...,\bar{f}_{300})
where
\bar{f}_{k}:=\frac{1}{50}\sum_{i=1}^{50}f(x_{k}^{(i)})-f(x_{*})
. Light colored area around the plots illustrate the standard deviation of the mean suboptimality.
8
8

8

More specifically, we highlight the region between
(\bar{f}_{0}\pm\sigma^{f}_{0},...,\bar{f}_{300}\pm\sigma^{f}_{300})
where
\sigma^{f}_{k}:=\big(\frac{1}{50}\sum_{i=1}^{50}|f(x_{k}^{(i)})-f(x_{*})|^{2}\big)^{1/2}
.
RA-AGD and RA- GMM performed similarly, where we see an overlap on their plots. These results illustrate that RA-AGD and RA- GMM can trade-off convergence rate with the entropic risk; both algorithms converge slower than GD and AGD; but achieve better asymptotic mean suboptimality. We also plotted the cumulative distribution of
\{f(x_{300}^{(i)})-f(x_{*})\}_{i=1}^{50}
on the right panel of Figure
4
.
We can observe from this figure that both of the risk-averse algorithms (RA- GMM and RA-AGD) have stochastic dominance over GD and AGD; that is, for any given
t>0
, the tail probability
\mathbb{P}\{f(x^{(i)}_{300})-f(x_{*})\geq t\}
of the samples
\{f(x_{300}^{(i)})\}_{i=1}^{50}
generated by risk averse algorithms is smaller than the the same probability of the ones generated by standard algorithms. This is expected as RA-AGD and RA- GMM minimizes the entropic risk subject to rate constraints and this leads to better control of the tail behavior of suboptimality. We also consider the
empirical finite-horizon entorpic risk
,

\tilde{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta):=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\frac{1}{50}\sum_{i=1}^{50}e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k}^{(i)})-f(x_{*}))},

(53)

which approximates the finite-horizon entropic risk
r_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
, of each of the algorithms. In Figure
5
, we illustrate the the convergence of empirical finite-horizon risk
\tilde{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
for
\theta=5
. According to Proposition
1
, finite-horizon risk converges to the infinite horizon risk with rate
\rho(A_{Q})
. As expected, GD and AGD converges to stationarity faster (as their
\rho(A_{Q})
is smaller) than RA-AGD and RA- GMM; however RA-AGD and RA- GMM have better asymptotic entropic risk as intended.

Figure 5:
The empirical finite-horizon risk measure,
\tilde{r}_{k,1}(5)
versus iterations on the quadratic optimization problem (
52
).

Next, we consider the regularized binary logistic regression problem for which the objective function
f
in (
1
) is

f(x)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\log(1+\exp\{-y_{i}(X_{i}^{\top}x)\})+\frac{\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle\mbox{reg}}}{2}\|x\|^{2},

where
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{100}
is the feature vector and
y_{i}\in\{-1,1\}
is the label of
i
-th sample. We set the regularization parameter
\lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle\mbox{reg}}=1
, the number of features
d=100
, and the sample size
N=1000
. We generated the synthetic data by sampling a random vector
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{100}
, where its each component,
\mathbf{w}_{i}
, is drawn from
\mathcal{N}(0,25)
. We also generated the vectors
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{100}
by sampling its each component from
\mathcal{N}(0,25)
for every
i=1,2,\dots,N
. We created the labels by
y_{i}=1
if
X_{i}^{\top}\mathbf{w}\geq 0
and
-1
otherwise. For the logistic regression, we calculated the parameters of the entropic risk-averse GMM (RA- GMM) by solving the optimization problem (
51
) for
\varphi=0.99
(used to estimate the EV@R bound),
\zeta=0.95
and
\varepsilon=0.05
on the sets using grid search for the parameters. For risk-averse AGD (RA-AGD), we choose
\vartheta=1=\psi
. Under this choice of parameters, the class of parameters
(\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi},\beta_{\vartheta,\psi},\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi})
we can choose simplifies and can be represented as

(\alpha,\frac{1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu}}{1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu}},\frac{1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu}}{1+\sqrt{\alpha\mu}})
where
\alpha\in(0,1/L]
. Therefore, we can see that the EV@R bound can be written as a function of
\alpha
only, i.e.,
\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}=\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha)
, and similarly the convergence rate can be written as
\rho_{AGD}^{2}(\alpha)=1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu}
. Therefore, the analogue of the problem (
51
) for RA-AGD becomes
\alpha\in(0,1/L]
to solve
\min_{\alpha\in(0,1/L]}\bar{E}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha)
subject to
\rho_{AGD}^{2}(\alpha)\leq(1+\varepsilon)\rho_{*}^{2}
where we take the same
\zeta=0.95
and
\varepsilon=0.05
to compare with RA- GMM. We use grid search over the parameter
\alpha
to solve this problem. On the left panel of Figure
6
, we compared the mean suboptimality of RA- GMM, RA-AGD, AGD and GD over 50 sample paths for
k=600
iterations.
9
9

9

For plotting the suboptimality, the optimal value
f(x_{*})
of the objective in logistic regression needs to be estimated, for this we run deterministic gradient descent on the logistic regression problem.
Similar to the quadratic example, we added standard deviations of the runs and started the iterations from
x_{0}=[1,...,1]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{100}
.
Our results in Figure
6
(on the left) shows that AGD is the fastest among the algorithms as expected; however, it yields the least accurate result asymptotically. GD is slower than AGD but with better asymptotic performance.
Both of the risk-averse algorithms converge slower than GD and AGD but they yield to better mean suboptimality subject to standard deviations of the suboptimality. This is expected from the formula (
8
) as the entropic risk captures both the mean suboptimality and the standard deviation of the suboptimality.

RA- GMM does have a slight improvement over RA-AGD. On the right panel of Figure
6
, we illustrate the cumulative distribution of the suboptimality on the last iterate. Similar to what we have observed on quadratic problem,
we can see that distributions corresponding to RA- GMM and RA-AGD have stochastic dominance over the distributions of GD and AGD. This illustrates that with RA-AGD and RA- GMM the probability that the suboptimality exceeds a threshold is smaller.

Lastly in Figure
7
, we plot the empirical finite-horizon entropic risk (see (
6
)) for logistic regression, taking
\theta=5
. For AGD and GMM, from (
38
) we expect the finite-horizon entropic risk to converge linearly with a rate proportional to the convergence rate
\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}^{2}
to an interval; for GD, based on(
38
), we also expect the same behavior with convergence rate
\rho_{GD}^{2}(\alpha)
. Figure
7
illustrates these results where we observe that RA-AGD and RA- GMM converges to a smaller asymptotic entropic risk value but at a slower pace as expected illustrating our theoretical results.

over iterations for the logistic regression problem.

6
Extensions to sub-Gaussian noise and to more general choice of parameters

In this section, we discuss how our results can be extended to hold under more general noise that have light tails. The same proof techniques as before will work, albeit some minor differences in the constants. In the following, we assume the noise, when conditioned on the iterates, have sub-Gaussian tails. Such noise structure have been commonly considered in the literature for studying stochastic gradient methods and their variants (see e.g.
[
40
,
55
,
56
]
). In empirical risk minimization problems such as the logistic regression problem we considered in Section
5
; if the gradients are estimated from randomly selected
b
data points, the noise will behave sub-Gaussian for
b
large enough due to the central limit theorem and this assumption will hold
[
56
]
. If the noise is uniformly bounded over iterations (with probability one); this assumption will also hold.

Assumption 2
.

For each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the gradient noise
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}=\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k})-\nabla f(y_{k})
is independent from the natural filtration
\mathcal{F}_{k}
generated by the iterates
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}
, it is centered and sub-Gaussian with a variance proxy
\sigma^{2}>0
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}~|~\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(54)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{\|\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}\|\geq t~|~\mathcal{F}_{k}\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2e^{-\frac{t^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}.

(55)

Theorem
2
assumed Gaussian noise and used the properties of the moment generating function of Gaussian distributions to integrate the noise. The next result, extends this result to the sub-Gaussian setting where the proof idea applies the same; except the bounds on the moment generating function of the noise needs to be adapted. The proof is in Appendix
C.1
. We obtain the same rate and the asymptotic suboptimality bound (the variance term) has the same dependency to the parameters and is larger by a constant factor of 2 compared to the Gaussian noise setting considered in Theorem
2
.

Proposition 5
.

Consider noisy GMM on
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with initialization
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}x_{0}^{\top}&x_{-1}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and with parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi},\beta_{\vartheta,\psi},\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi})
as given in Theorem
2
. Under Assumption
2
, the expected suboptimality satisfies the bound

\mathbb{E}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho^{2k}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0})+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{\sigma^{2}\alpha^{2}}{1-\rho^{2}}(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}),

(56)

where
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}}
and
\rho^{2}=\rho^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}
as in
2
.

Next, we provide bounds on the finite/infinite-horizon entropic risk. The results look similar to Proposition
3
where
\theta
needs to be chosen smaller; and the entropic risk bounds we obtain are a bit larger
10
10

10

This follows from comparing the formulae (
58
) and (
38
) with the observation that, for
\theta<\theta_{u}^{sg}
, we have
\frac{1}{2-\theta\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}(\vartheta+\alpha L)}\leq\frac{1}{2-\frac{1}{4}}=\frac{4}{7}
and

\bar{\rho}^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}\leq\hat{\rho}^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}
.
. This is expected as we consider more general sub-Gaussian noise (that contains Gaussian noise as a special case). The proof is in the Appendix
C.2
.

Proposition 6
.

Consider noisy GMM on
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with initialization
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}=\begin{bmatrix}x_{0}^{\top}&x_{-1}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}
and with parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi},\beta_{\vartheta,\psi},\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi})
as given in Theorem
2
. Under
Assumption
2
, if the risk-averseness parameter
\theta
satisfies the condition

\theta<\theta_{u}^{sg}:=\min\left\{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1-\rho^{2}}{64\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}},\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{1}{4\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}\right\},

(57)

where
\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu}(2(\beta-\gamma)^{2}+(1-\alpha L)^{2}(1+2\gamma+2\gamma^{2}))+\lambda_{P}\rho^{2}
with
\rho=\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}
and
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}}
. The finite-horizon entropic risk measure of the algorithm admits the bound

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)<\frac{4\sigma^{2}\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}{1-\hat{\rho}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}}+\hat{\rho}^{2k}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}2\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}),

(58)

where

for
\tilde{P}
defined in (
34
) and the rate
\hat{\rho}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}<1
is given as

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\rho}^{2}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\rho^{2}+32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta\right)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\rho^{2}+32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta)^{2}+128\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta}.

(59)

Similarly, based on the results of Proposition

, we can obtain bounds on EV@R that extends Theorem

to the sub-Gaussian noise, the proof is deferred to the Appendix.

Theorem 5
.

Consider the noisy GMM on
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
where the parameters are set as

\alpha=\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}
,
\beta=\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}

and the noise on the gradient admits Assumption
2
. Fix an arbitrary

and confidence level

. Consider
\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}:=\varphi\theta_{u}^{sg}
and

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{t}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}:=\sqrt{(\rho^{2}+32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta_{\varphi}^{sg})^{2}+128\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}},

where
\rho=\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}
and
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}}
.
If the confidence level satisfies the condition,

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\log(1/\zeta)\leq 128\alpha^{4}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(L+2\lambda_{P})\Big(\frac{\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}}{2-\rho^{2}-\hat{t}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}-\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha\beta,\gamma}}\Big)^{2},

(60)

for
\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
defined in Proposition
6
,
then the EV@R of
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
admits the bound

EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq}\frac{\sigma^{2}\alpha^{2}}{2-\rho^{2}-\hat{t}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}}\Big(\sqrt{8(L+2\lambda_{P})}+\sqrt{64\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\log(1/\zeta)}\Big)^{2}+(\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})^{k}2\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}).

(61)

In the case (
60
) does not hold, we have

EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq}\frac{8\sigma^{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho^{2}-\hat{t}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}-32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}}+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}}\log(1/\zeta)+(\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})^{k}2\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}),

(62)

where the rate
\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}:=\frac{1}{2}\big(\rho^{2}+32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{(\rho^{2}+32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta_{\varphi}^{sg})^{2}+128\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}}.

(63)

We have also

\underset{k\rightarrow\infty}{\lim\sup}\;EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})]\\
\leq\hat{E}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma):=\begin{cases}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{\sigma^{2}\alpha^{2}}{2-\rho^{2}-\hat{t}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}}\Big(\sqrt{8(L+2\lambda_{P})}+\sqrt{64\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\log(1/\zeta)}\Big)^{2},&\text{ if \eqref{cond: conf-lev-subgauss} holds},\\
\frac{8\sigma^{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho^{2}-\hat{t}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}-32\alpha^{2}\hat{\mathtt{v}}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}}+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta_{\varphi}^{sg}}\log(1/\zeta),&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

(64)

Corollary 3
.

Consider the noisy GMM in the premise of Theorem
5
. The tail of the distribution
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
at

-th iteration admits the inequality

\mathbb{P}\{f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\geq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{E}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)+(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma})^{k}2\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0})\}\leq\zeta,

(65)

where
\hat{\hat{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}
as given in (
40
). Moreover, we have the following for the limiting distribution of noisy GMM

\mathbb{P}\{f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\geq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hat{E}_{1-\zeta}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\}\leq\zeta.

(66)

Proof.

We omit the proof since it is similar to the proof of Corollary

∎

Remark 6
.

Proposition
5
, Proposition
6
, and Theorem
5
provide explicit bounds for particular choice of parameters based on constructing explicit solutions to the (
MI
) provided in Appendix
B
. However, if we do not seek for explicit bounds, then our results
can be extended to more general choice of parameters than the ones provided in Theorem
2
. In particular, suppose there exist a choice of parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
, a positive semi-definite matrix
\tilde{P}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a convergence rate
\rho^{2}\in(0,1)
which satisfies the matrix inequality (
MI
). If such parameters,
\tilde{P}
and
\rho^{2}
can be found numerically, then
the results of Proposition
5
, Proposition
6
, and Theorem
5
will hold verbatim if
we take
\lambda_{P}
to be the largest eigenvalue of the
d
-by-
d
principle minor matrix of
P:=\tilde{P}\otimes I_{d}
and use the parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
and the rate
\rho^{2}
that satisfies the (
MI
).

7
Conclusion

In this work, we considered generalized momentum methods (GMM) subject to random gradient noise. We study the convergence rate and entropic risk of suboptimality as a function of the parameters. For both quadratic objectives and strongly convex smooth objectives, we obtained a number of new non-asymptotic convergence results when the noise is light-tailed (Gaussian or sub-Gaussian) as a function of the parameters. Furthermore, we provided explicit bounds on the entropic risk and entropic value at risk of suboptimality at a given iterate as well as the probability that the suboptimality exceeds a given threshold. For quadratic objectives, we also obtained (explicit) sharp characterizations of the entropic risk and the set of parameters for which the entropic risk is finite. Our results highlight trade-offs between the convergence rate and the entropic risk. We also introduced risk-averse GMM (RA- GMM) which can select the parameters to trade-off the convergence rate and the entropic value at risk in a systematic manner. Finally, we provided numerical experiments which illustrate our result and show that RA- GMM leads to improved tail behavior.

Acknowledgements
This work was partially funded by the grants ONR N00014-21-1-2244, NSF CCF-1814888, and NSF DMS-2053485.

Appendix A
Proofs of Section
3

A.1
Proof of Lemma
3.1

The result follows from the Jordan decomposition of the matrix
A_{Q}
. By arguments similar to
[
57
]
, there exists an orthonormal matrix
U
such that

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}UA_{Q}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}=\text{blkdiag}(\{M_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}),

where
M_{i}
is the
2\times 2
matrix

M_{i}:=\begin{bmatrix}(1+\beta)-\alpha(1+\gamma)\lambda_{i}&-(\beta-\alpha\gamma\lambda_{i})\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}.

(67)

Recall that

c_{i}=(1+\beta)-\alpha(1+\gamma)\lambda_{i},\quad d_{i}=-(\beta-\alpha\gamma\lambda_{i}),

M_{i}
admits the Jordan decomposition
M_{i}=V_{i}J_{i}V_{i}^{-1}
where

V_{i}=\begin{cases}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{c_{i}-\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}&\frac{c_{i}+\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}\\
1&1\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
\begin{bmatrix}\frac{c_{i}}{2}&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0,\end{cases}

V_{i}^{-1}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}\begin{bmatrix}-1&\frac{c_{i}+\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}\\
1&-\frac{c_{i}-\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\neq}0,\\
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&\frac{-c_{i}}{2}\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0,\end{cases}

J_{i}=\begin{cases}\begin{bmatrix}\frac{c_{i}-\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}&0\\
0&\frac{c_{i}+\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
\begin{bmatrix}\frac{c_{i}}{2}&1\\
0&\frac{c_{i}}{2}\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0.\end{cases}

We observe that if
c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0
, then
M_{i}
has a double eigenvalue with a corresponding Jordan block of size 2, otherwise
M_{i}
is diagonalizable with two different eigenvalues (Jordan blocks are of size 1). It follows that

M_{i}^{k}=V_{i}J_{i}^{k}V_{i}^{-1},

where

J_{i}^{k}=\begin{cases}\begin{bmatrix}\left(\frac{c_{i}-\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}\right)^{k}&0\\
0&\left(\frac{c_{i}+\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}}}{2}\right)^{k}\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
\begin{bmatrix}(\frac{c_{i}}{2})^{k}&k(\frac{c_{i}}{2})^{k-1}\\
0&(\frac{c_{i}}{2})^{k}\end{bmatrix},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0.\end{cases}

Then, we have

\displaystyle\|A_{Q}^{k}\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}\text{blkdiag}(\{M_{i}^{k}\}_{i=1}^{n})\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U\|,

(68)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|M_{i}^{k}\|,

(69)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|V_{i}\|\|J_{i}^{k}\|\|V_{i}^{-1}\|,

(70)

where we used
\|P\|=1
. Notice that

\displaystyle\rho(J_{i})=\rho_{i}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}|c_{i}|+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}},&\text{ if }c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}>0,\\
\sqrt{|d_{i}|},&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

We have also

\rho(A_{Q})=\max_{i}\rho_{i}

by definition. If
c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0
then
J_{i}^{k}
is diagonal and we obtain

\|J_{i}^{k}\|\leq{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{i}^{k}}\quad\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0.

(71)

Otherwise, if
c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0
, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\frac{c_{i}}{2}=}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{i}}
and

\|J_{i}^{k}\|_{2}\leq\|J_{i}^{k}\|_{F}:=\sqrt{2(\frac{c_{i}}{2})^{2k}+{k^{2}(\frac{c_{i}}{2})^{2k-2}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=}\sqrt{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\rho_{i}^{2k}+k^{2}\rho_{i}^{2k-2}}},

(72)

where
\|\cdot\|_{F}
denotes the Frobenius norm. In any case, we have

\|J_{i}^{k}\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2\big(\rho(A_{Q})\big)^{2}+k^{2}}\rho(A_{Q})^{k-1}\mbox{ for every }i,

(73)

where we used
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho_{i}}\leq\rho(A_{Q})
. Moreover,

\|V_{i}\|_{2}\leq\|V_{i}\|_{F}:=\begin{cases}\sqrt{|c_{i}^{2}+2d_{i}+2|},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
\sqrt{\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{4}+2},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0.\end{cases}

(74)

Similarly,

\|V_{i}^{-1}\|_{2}\leq\|V_{i}^{-1}\|_{F}:=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\frac{|c_{i}^{2}+2d_{i}+2|}{|c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}|}},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
\sqrt{\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{4}+2},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0.\end{cases}

(75)

Therefore,

\|V_{i}\|_{2}\|V_{i}^{-1}\|_{2}\leq\begin{cases}\frac{|c_{i}^{2}+2d_{i}+2|}{\sqrt{|c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}|}},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{4}+2,&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0.\end{cases}

Putting everything together, we conclude from (
15
) that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\|\mathbb{E}[z_{k}-z_{*}]\|\leq\|A_{Q}^{k}\|\|z_{0}-z_{*}\|},

where

\displaystyle\|A_{Q}^{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\max_{i=1,2,\dots,d}\begin{cases}\frac{|c_{i}^{2}+2d_{i}+2|}{\sqrt{|c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}|}}\rho_{i}^{k},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
(\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{4}+2)\sqrt{2\rho_{i}^{2k}+k^{2}\rho_{i}^{2k-2}},&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0,\end{cases}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\rho(A_{Q})^{k-1}\max_{i=1,2,\dots,d}\begin{cases}\frac{|c_{i}^{2}+2d_{i}+2|\rho_{i}}{\sqrt{|c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}|}}&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}\neq 0,\\
(\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{4}+2)\sqrt{2\rho_{i}^{2}+k^{2}}&\mbox{if}\quad c_{i}^{2}+4d_{i}=0.\end{cases}

This completes the proof.

A.2
Proof of Proposition
1

Before proving Proposition
1
, we first give the characterization of eigenvalues of the matrix
Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}
, where
\Sigma_{\infty}
is the limiting covariance matrix defined as
\Sigma_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[(x_{k}-\mathbb{E}[x_{k}])(x_{k}-\mathbb{E}[x_{k}])^{\top}]
.

Lemma A.1
.

Under the setting of Proposition
1
, the eigenvalues of the matrix
Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}
where
\Sigma_{\infty}
is the limiting covariance matrix
\Sigma_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[(x_{k}-\mathbb{E}[x_{k}])(x_{k}-\mathbb{E}[x_{k}])^{\top}]
admit the form

\lambda_{i}\left(Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}\right)=\frac{\lambda_{i}(Q)(1-d_{i})\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}}{(1+d_{i})[(1-d_{i})^{2}-c_{i}^{2}]},

for
i=1,2,\dots,d
.

Proof.

The proof is given in Appendix
D.1
.
∎

Under the assumption on the noise each iteration
x_{k}
is Gaussian with
\mu_{k}:=\mathbb{E}[x_{k}]
and covariance matrix
\Sigma_{k}:=\mathbb{E}[(x_{k}-\mu_{k})(x_{k}-\mu_{k})^{\top}]
. Notice that the following recursion holds for the covariance matrix
\Xi_{k}
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}
under Assumption
1
(see also the proof of Lemma
A.1
),

\displaystyle\Xi_{k+1}=A_{Q}\Xi_{k}A_{Q}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}BB^{\top}.

(76)

Moreover, for all
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
the rate satisfies
\rho(A_{Q})<1
since
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
by Lemma
E.1
. Therefore, we can see that Lyapunov equation given above is stable, i.e.
\rho(A_{Q})<1
; hence,
there exists
\Xi_{\infty}
such that
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\Xi_{k}=\Xi_{\infty}
which also satisfies the Lyapunov equation
\Xi_{\infty}=A_{Q}\Xi_{\infty}A^{\top}_{Q}+\sigma^{2}BB^{\top}
. Therefore, we can define the matrix
\Sigma_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\Sigma_{k}
which is the d-by-d principle minor of the matrix
\Xi_{\infty}
. Since the iterates
\{x_{k}\}_{k\geq 0}
admit normal distribution
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{k},\Sigma_{k})
, we can write,

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R_{k}(\theta):=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))\}\right]=n_{k}\int\exp\{\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}}(x-x_{*})^{\top}Q(x-x_{*})\}\exp\{-\frac{1}{2}(x-\mu_{k})^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}(x-\mu_{k})\}dx,

where
n_{k}:=(2\pi)^{-\frac{d}{2}}det(\Sigma_{k})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. Notice that

(x-x_{*})^{\top}Q(x-x_{*})=(x-\mu_{k}+\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}Q(x-\mu_{k}+\mu_{k}-x_{*}).

Hence we write

\displaystyle\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R_{k}(\theta)

\displaystyle=n_{k}\int\Big(e^{\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}}(x-\mu_{k})^{\top}Q(x-\mu_{k})+\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}}{2\sigma^{2}}(x-\mu_{k})^{\top}Q(\mu_{k}-x_{*})}+e^{\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}Q(\mu_{k}-x_{*})-\frac{1}{2}(x-\mu_{k})^{\top}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}(x-\mu_{k})}\Big)dx,

\displaystyle=n_{k}e^{\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}Q(\mu_{k}-x_{*})}\int e^{\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}}{2\sigma^{2}}(x-\mu_{k})^{\top}Q(\mu_{k}-x_{*})-\frac{1}{2}(x-\mu_{k})^{\top}[\Sigma_{k}^{-1}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q](x-\mu_{k})}dx,

\displaystyle=n_{k}e^{\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}Q(\mu_{k}-x_{*})+\frac{\theta^{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}8}\sigma^{4}}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}Q^{1/2}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{1/2}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})}\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}(x-v_{k})^{\top}Q^{1/2}M_{k}Q^{1/2}(x-v_{k})}dx,

where
M_{k}=Q^{-1/2}\Sigma_{k}^{-1}Q^{-1/2}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}I
and
v_{k}=\mu_{k}+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{-1/2}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{1/2}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})
.
We can see from the characterization of eigenvalues of
\lambda_{i}(Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2})
given at Lemma
A.1
and the definition of
u_{i}
that
Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}\prec\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}I_{d}
for all
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
. Moreover, the covariance matrix
\Sigma_{k}
satisfies
\Sigma_{k}\preceq\Sigma_{\infty}
for each
k\geq 0
which we prove later in Lemma
A.2
. Therefore, we also obtain the property that
Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}\prec\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}I_{d}
. Consequently, both
M_{k}
and
M_{\infty}
are invertible.
The integral on the right-hand side of the last equality can be considered as a multidimensional Gaussian integral which can be computed as

\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}(x-v_{k})^{\top}Q^{1/2}M_{k}Q^{1/2}(x-v_{k})}dx=\begin{cases}(2\pi)^{d/2}\det(Q^{-1/2}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{-1/2})^{1/2},&\text{if }\lambda_{\min}\{Q^{-1/2}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{-1/2}\}>0,\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

(77)

where
\lambda_{\min}
denotes the smallest eigenvalue. Since
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
, it can be seen that the integral (
77
) is finite which yields

\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))}]=\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{k}^{1/2})^{-1/2}e^{\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}\left[Q+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\right](\mu_{k}-x_{*})}.

Hence we can compute the entropic risk,

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}\left[Q+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\right](\mu_{k}-x_{*})-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}).

In the following, we adopt the notation
\mu_{\infty}:=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu_{k}
. Since
\Sigma_{\infty}
exists by our arguments above, we can see that the infinite horizon entropic risk measure is of the form

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}[\exp\{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\}],

where
x_{\infty}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{\infty},\Sigma_{\infty})
.
Notice that we can compute
\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
by writing

R_{\infty}(\theta)=\mathbb{E}[\exp\{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})\}],

for
x_{\infty}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{\infty},\Sigma_{\infty})
. Then, by following the arguments we made for
\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
, we obtain

R_{\infty}(\theta)=n_{\infty}e^{\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{\infty}-x_{*})^{\top}Q(\mu_{\infty}-x_{*})+\frac{\theta^{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}8}\sigma^{4}}(\mu_{\infty}-x_{*})^{\top}Q^{1/2}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}(\mu_{\infty}-x_{*})}\times\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}(x-v_{\infty})^{\top}Q^{1/2}M_{\infty}Q^{1/2}(x-v_{\infty})}dx,

where
n_{\infty}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}n_{k}
and
v_{\infty}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}v_{k}
. If we substitute
v_{k}
and
\Sigma_{k}
with
v_{\infty}
and
\Sigma_{\infty}
at (
77
), we can see from the eigenvalues of
Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}
provided in Lemma
A.1
that the infinite-horizon risk measure is finite if and only if
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
. After showing the existence of infinite-horizon risk measure, we are next going to show the linear convergence of
r_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
to
r_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
. The difference between finite-horizon and infinite-horizon risk is

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)-\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2})-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{k}^{1/2})\\
+(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}\left[Q+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\right](\mu_{k}-x_{*})\\
-(\mu_{\infty}-x_{*})^{\top}\left[Q+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\right](\mu_{\infty}-x_{*}).

Since
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
by Lemma
E.1
, the equality
\mu_{\infty}=x_{*}
directly follows from Lemma
3.1
. Adding and subtracting
\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}Q^{1/2}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})
leads to,

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)-\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2})-\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{k}^{1/2})\\
+(\mu_{k}-x_{*})^{\top}\left[Q+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\left(M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}^{-1}\right)Q^{1/2}\right](\mu_{k}-x_{*})\\
+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}(\mu_{k}-\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x_{*})^{\top}Q^{1/2}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}(\mu_{k}-x_{*}).

The matrices
Q,M_{k}
and
M_{\infty}
are all symmetric matrices. From the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality;

|\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)-\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)|\leq\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}|\log\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2})-\log\det(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{k}^{1/2})|\\
+\|Q+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\|_{2}\|\mu_{k}-x_{*}\|^{2}\\
+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}\|M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}^{-1}\|_{2}\|Q^{1/2}(\mu_{k}-x_{*})\|^{2}.\vskip-5.69046pt

(78)

Recall the following result from Lemma
3.1
,

\|\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}\|^{2}\leq C_{k}^{2}\rho(A_{Q})^{2(k-1)}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}\|^{2},

where
C_{k}
is as given in Lemma
3.1
. Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}^{\top}=[x_{k}^{\top},x_{k-1}^{\top}]^{\top}
, we also obtain

\|\mu_{k}-x_{*}\|^{2}\leq\|\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}]-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}\|^{2}\leq C_{k}^{2}\rho(A_{Q})^{2(k-1)}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*}\|^{2}.

(79)

On the other hand, the difference
\Xi_{k}-\Xi_{\infty}
also satisfies the recursion,

\Xi_{k}-\Xi_{\infty}=A_{Q}\left(\Xi_{k-1}-\Xi_{\infty}\right)A_{Q}^{\top}.

(80)

We introduce the notation

vec(X^{\prime})=[X^{\prime}_{11},X^{\prime}_{21},...,X^{\prime}_{d1},X^{\prime}_{12},...,X^{\prime}_{dd}],

to denote the vectorization
vec(X^{\prime})
of a matrix
X^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
.
Under this notation, (
80
) can be rewritten,

vec(\Xi_{k}-\Xi_{\infty})=vec\left(A_{Q}^{k}(\Xi_{0}-\Xi_{\infty})(A_{Q}^{\top})^{k}\right)=(A_{Q}^{k}\otimes A_{Q}^{k})vec(\Xi_{0}-\Xi_{\infty}),

where
\otimes
denotes the Kronecker product.
Hence,

\|\Xi_{k}-\Xi_{\infty}\|_{F}^{2}=\|vec(\Xi_{k}-\Xi_{\infty})\|^{2}=\|A_{Q}^{k}\|_{2}^{4}\|vec(\Xi_{0}-\Xi_{\infty})\|^{2},

(81)

where we used the fact that
\|A_{Q}^{k}\otimes A_{Q}^{k}\|_{2}^{2}=\|A_{Q}^{k}\|_{2}^{4}
(see e.g.
[
58
]
). Notice that
\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty}\|^{2}_{F}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq\|\Xi_{k}-\Xi_{\infty}\|^{2}_{F}
since
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}^{\top}=[x_{k}^{\top},x_{k-1}^{\top}]
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{\infty}=[x_{\infty}^{\top},x_{\infty}^{\top}]^{\top}
. Hence,

\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty}\|^{2}_{F}\leq\|A_{Q}^{k}\|^{4}_{2}\|vec(\Xi_{0}-\Xi_{\infty})\|^{2}_{2}.

(82)

Now, we are going to provide the convergence rate for the log-determinant term on (
78
); but first, we provide the following property of the log-determinant:

\displaystyle\log\det(X^{\prime})-\log\det(Y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\log\det((Y^{\prime})^{-1/2}X^{\prime}(Y^{\prime})^{-1/2}),

\displaystyle\leq\text{Tr}((Y^{\prime})^{-1/2}X^{\prime}(Y^{\prime})^{-1/2}-I),

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}((Y^{\prime})^{-1}(X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}))=vec((Y^{\prime})^{-1})^{\top}vec(X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}),

(83)

for any positive definite matrices
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
. In Lemma
A.2
, we show that
X
and
Y
are positive definite matrices; so that we can apply the equality (
A.2
) with
X=X^{\prime}
and
Y=Y^{\prime}
.

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
. Then, the matrices
X=I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}
and
Y=I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}
are positive definite for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover,
\Sigma_{k}\preceq\Sigma_{\infty}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

The proof is deferred to Appendix
D.2

∎

Lemma
A.2
implies that the matrices
X
and
Y
are positive-definite since
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
by assumption of Proposition
1
; therefore, the inequality (
A.2
) holds for
X^{\prime}=X
and
Y^{\prime}=Y
. Notice that the matrices
\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{k}^{1/2}
and
\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}
share the same eigenvalues with
Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}
and
Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}
, respectively. On the other hand, we also have the eigenvalue characterization of the log-determinant which implies
\log\det(X^{\prime})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\lambda_{i}(X^{\prime})
where
\lambda_{i}(X^{\prime})
is the
i
-th largest eigenvalue of the positive -definite matrix
X^{\prime}
. Hence we can write

\log\det(X)-\log\det(Y)=\log\det\left(I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}\right)-\log\det\left(I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}\right),

and the following inequalities hold by applying the inequality (
A.2
) for
X^{\prime}=I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}
and
Y^{\prime}=I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}
, and using Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\left|\log\det(X)-\log\det(Y)\right|\leq\Big\|vec((I-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2})^{-1})\Big\|\Big\|vec\left(Q^{1/2}(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty})Q^{1/2}\right)\Big\|.

The Frobenius norm satisfies the property
\|vec(X^{\prime})\|^{2}=\|X^{\prime}\|^{2}_{F}
for any matrix
X^{\prime}
, and we can also write
vec(Q^{1/2}(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty})Q^{1/2})=(Q^{1/2}\otimes Q^{1/2})vec(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty})
using the Kronecker product. Therefore, the inequality above becomes

\displaystyle\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\left|\log\det(X)-\log\det(Y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\Big\|(I-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2})^{-1}\Big\|_{F}\Big\|Q^{1/2}\otimes Q^{1/2}\Big\|_{2}\Big\|vec(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty})\Big\|,

\displaystyle\leq L\Big\|(I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2})\Big\|_{F}\Big\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty}\Big\|_{F},

(84)

where we used the fact that eigenvalues of
Q^{1/2}\otimes Q^{1/2}
are
\lambda_{i}(Q)^{1/2}\lambda_{j}(Q)^{1/2}
for
i=1,2,...,d
and
j=1,2,...,d
where
\lambda_{i}(Q)
is the
i
-th largest eigenvalue of
Q
11
11

11

See e.g.
[
59
]
for details.
. Combining (
84
) with the inequality (
82
) yields that the log-determinant term of (
78
) converges linearly , i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\left|\log\det(X)-\log\det(Y)\right|\leq L\|(I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2}Q\Sigma_{\infty}^{1/2})^{-1}\|_{F}\|A_{Q}^{k}\|_{2}^{2}\|\Xi_{0}-\Xi_{\infty}\|_{F}.

(85)

Next, we are going to show that
M_{k}^{-1}
converges linearly to
M_{\infty}^{-1}
. We can write the following equality for any invertible matrix
M
:

\displaystyle I=(\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}I+M)(\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}I+M)^{-1}\implies M^{-1}=\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}M^{-1}(\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}I+M)^{-1}+(\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}I+M)^{-1}.

Setting
M=M_{k}
and
M=M_{\infty}
yields the following relations,

\displaystyle M_{k}^{-1}=\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}+Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},

\displaystyle M_{\infty}^{-1}=\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}+Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}.

Therefore the difference
M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}^{-1}
is,

M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}^{-1}=\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}M_{k}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}+Q^{1/2}(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty})Q^{1/2},

Adding and subtracting the term
\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}M_{\infty}^{-1}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}
leads to

\displaystyle M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}^{-1}=\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}^{-1})Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}+\left(I+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}M_{\infty}^{-1}\right)Q^{1/2}(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty})Q^{1/2}.

Hence, solving this linear system for
M_{k}^{-1}
,

M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}^{-1}=\left(I+\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}M_{\infty}^{-1}\right)Q^{1/2}(\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty})Q^{1/2}\left(I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}\right)^{-1}.

Notice that the condition
\Sigma_{k}\preceq\Sigma_{\infty}
(from Lemma
A.2
) and
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
imply
I-\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{k}Q^{1/2}\succeq I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}\succ 0
; hence, we get

\|M_{k}^{-1}-M_{\infty}\|_{2}\leq D_{\infty}\|\Sigma_{k}-\Sigma_{\infty}\|_{2},

(86)

where
D_{\infty}=\|A_{Q}\|_{2}\|I+\frac{\theta}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}\sigma^{2}}M_{\infty}^{-1}\|_{2}\|I-\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}Q^{1/2}\Sigma_{\infty}Q^{1/2}\|^{-1}
.
Combining everything together, the bound (
78
) with inequalities (
79
), (
82
), (
85
), and (
86
) yields the desired result.

A.3
Proof of Proposition
2

Consider the dynamical system (
25
) that the transformed iterates
\bar{z}_{k}
obey with the output vector
\bar{q}_{k}
defined as,

\displaystyle\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=A_{\Lambda}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k}+B{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}},

(87)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{k}

\displaystyle=S\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{k},

(88)

where
S=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left[\Lambda^{1/2},0_{d}\right]
. The entropic risk measure of GMM under standard Gaussian noise
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d})
is

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]}\right]=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{\infty}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{\infty}}\right],

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{\infty}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{k}
is a normally distributed random variable with mean
0
and the matrix
V_{\infty}:=\mathbb{E}[\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{\infty}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{\infty}^{\top}]
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{\infty}^{\top}]=\mathbb{E}[S\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{\infty}\bar{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}}_{\infty}^{\top}S^{\top}]=SV_{\infty}S^{\top}.

Furthermore, the matrix
V_{\infty}
also satisfies the Lyapunov equation (
27
); hence we can write

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}V_{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}A_{\Lambda}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U\left(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}V_{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U\right)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}A_{\Lambda}^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U+\sigma^{2}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}BB^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U,

where
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U
is the projection matrix given in (
67
) which satisfies
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U=I
. We introduce
\tilde{V}_{\infty}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}V_{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U
,
\tilde{B}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}B
, and
\tilde{A}_{\Lambda}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}A_{\Lambda}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U
, and rewrite the Lyapunov equation as

\tilde{V}_{\infty}=\tilde{A}_{\Lambda}\tilde{V}_{\infty}\tilde{A}_{\Lambda}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}\tilde{B}\tilde{B}^{\top},

(89)

where
\tilde{A}_{\Lambda}=\underset{i=1,..,d}{\mbox{Diag}}(\{M_{i}\})
where

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}M_{i}:=\begin{bmatrix}(1+\beta)-\alpha(1+\gamma)\lambda_{i}&-(\beta-\alpha\gamma\lambda_{i})\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},

and
\lambda_{i}:=\lambda_{i}(Q)
drops the dependency of the eigenvalues to
Q
for notational simplicity. The solution
\tilde{V}_{\infty}=\underset{i=1,..,d}{\mbox{Diag}}\begin{bmatrix}\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}}&\bar{y}_{\lambda_{i}}\\
\bar{y}_{\lambda_{i}}&\bar{v}_{\lambda_{i}}\end{bmatrix}
of the Lyapunov equation (
89
) satisfies the system of equations:

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle=c_{i}^{2}\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}}+2c_{i}d_{i}\bar{y}_{\lambda_{i}}+d_{i}^{2}\bar{v}_{\lambda_{i}}+\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2},

\displaystyle\bar{y}_{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle=c_{i}\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}}+d_{i}\bar{y}_{\lambda_{i}},

\displaystyle\bar{v}_{\lambda_{i}}

\displaystyle=\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}},

where
c_{i}=1+\beta-\alpha(1+\gamma)\lambda_{i}
and
d_{i}=-\beta+\alpha\gamma\lambda_{i}
. The solution of this system is given as

\displaystyle\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}}=\frac{(1-d_{i})\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}}{(1+d_{i})[(1-d_{i})^{2}-c_{i}^{2}]},\;\;\bar{y}_{\lambda_{i}}=\frac{c_{i}\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}}{(1+d_{i})[(1-d_{i})^{2}-c_{i}^{2}]},\;\;\bar{v}_{\lambda_{i}}=\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}},

From the definition of
\tilde{V}_{\infty}
, we have
\lambda_{i}(SV_{\infty}S^{\top})=\lambda_{i}(S\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U\tilde{V}_{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}S^{\top})
where the latter matrix can be written as

\displaystyle S\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U\tilde{V}_{\infty}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}U^{\top}S^{\top}

\displaystyle=\underset{i=1,..,d}{\mbox{Diag}}\left\{\frac{1}{2}\lambda_{i}\bar{x}_{\lambda_{i}}\right\}.

It directly follows that,

\lambda_{i}(SV_{\infty}S^{\top})=\frac{\lambda_{i}(1-d_{i})\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2(1+d_{i})[(1-d_{i})^{2}-c_{i}^{2}].}

Recall that for all
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
, by definition, we already have the property,

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda_{i}(SV_{\infty}S^{\top})=\frac{\lambda_{i}(1-d_{i})\alpha^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2(1+d_{i})[(1-d_{i})^{2}-c_{i}^{2}]}=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2u_{i}}<\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\theta}.

(90)

Therefore
\lambda_{\max}(SV_{\infty}S^{\top})<\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\theta}
from the condition, where
\lambda_{\max}(.)
denotes the largest eigenvalue.
On the other hand,
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{\infty}
is a Gaussian variable with covariance matrix
SV_{\infty}S^{\top}
; hence, it can be shown
12
12

12

It suffices to choose
m=0
,
\alpha^{2}(\lambda_{P}+L/2)=1
, and
\Sigma=SV_{\infty}S
in (
128
).
using the proof of Lemma
B.4
that

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\bar{q}}_{\infty}\|^{2}}]=\begin{cases}\det\left(I_{d}-\frac{\theta}{\sigma^{2}}SV_{\infty}S^{\top}\right)^{-1/2},&\text{ if }\lambda_{\max}(SV_{\infty}S^{\top})<\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\theta},\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}}

where we used
\lambda_{\max}(SV_{\infty}S^{\top})<\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\theta}
on
\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
, this yields the finite-horizon risk measure is finite if and only if
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{F}_{\theta}
and given as

\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)=\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{d}\log\left(1-\frac{\theta}{\sigma^{2}}\lambda_{i}\left(SV_{\infty}S^{\top}\right)\right)^{-1/2},

(91)

and we conclude from (
90
).

A.4
Proof of Theorem
1

Given
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)\in\mathcal{S}_{q}
, by Proposition
2
and the definition of EV@R,

EV@R_{1-\zeta}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\inf_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0<\theta}\;\mathbf{r}_{\sigma^{2}}(\theta)+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(1/\zeta)\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq\min_{0<\theta<2\bar{u}}\left\{-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\log(1-\frac{\theta}{2u_{i}})+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(1/\zeta)\right\}.

Notice that
\bar{u}\leq u_{i}
for every
i
which implies
-\log(1-\frac{\theta}{2u_{i}})\leq-\log(1-\frac{\theta}{2\bar{u}})
; therefore, we obtain an upper bound on the EV@R as follows:

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}EV@R_{1-\zeta}[f(x_{\infty})-f(x_{*})]\leq\min_{0<\theta<2\bar{u}}\left\{h(\theta)\right\},}

(92)

where
h(\theta):=-\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{\theta}\log(1-\frac{\theta}{2\bar{u}})+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(1/\zeta)
and its derivative
h^{\prime}(\theta)
is given as

\displaystyle h^{\prime}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{\theta^{2}}\left[\log\left(1-\frac{\theta}{2\bar{u}}\right)+\frac{2\bar{u}}{2\bar{u}-\theta}-1-\frac{2\log(1/\zeta)}{d}\right].

(93)

A simple explicit formula for
\theta_{*}
satisfying
h^{\prime}(\theta_{*})=0
does not appear to exist which would be an optimizer of (
92
); therefore, we are going to approximate
h^{\prime}(\theta)
by using
-\log(1-x)\approx x
for small
x
to find an approximate root of (
93
):

h^{\prime}(\theta)\approx\tilde{h}^{\prime}(\theta):=\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{\theta^{2}}\left[-\frac{\theta}{2\bar{u}}+\frac{2\bar{u}}{2\bar{u}-\theta}-(1+\frac{2\log(1/\zeta)}{d})\right].

The solution
\tilde{\theta}_{0}
of
\tilde{h}^{\prime}(\tilde{\theta}_{0})=0
on
(0,2\bar{u})
is the positive root of the polynomial

\theta^{2}+\frac{4\bar{u}\log(1/\zeta)}{d}\theta-\frac{8\bar{u}^{2}\log(1/\zeta)}{d}=0,

which is given as

\tilde{\theta}_{0}=\frac{2\bar{u}\log(1/\zeta)}{d}\left[-1+\sqrt{1+\frac{2d}{\log(1/\zeta)}}\right].

Notice the following relation holds

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+\frac{2d}{\log(1/\zeta)}}<1+\frac{d}{\log(1/\zeta)}\implies\frac{\log(1/\zeta)}{d}\left[-1+\sqrt{1+\frac{2d}{\log(1/\zeta)}}\right]<1.

Therefore
\tilde{\theta}_{0}=\theta_{0}2\bar{u}<2\bar{u}
; hence, we conclude from (
92
) that
EV@R_{1-\zeta}\leq h(\tilde{\theta_{0}})=h(\theta_{0}2\bar{u})
and obtain the inequality (
29
).

Appendix B
Proofs of Section
4

We first provide a result from the literature about deterministic GMM.

Lemma B.1

(
[
20
]
)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{S}_{\mu}^{L}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the iterates generated by the deterministic generalized momentum method, i.e. (
3
) with
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k}}=0
for every
k
. Suppose there exists a symmetric positive semi-definite matrix
\tilde{P}
and a rate
\rho<1
for which the following matrix inequality holds

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\tilde{A}^{\top}\tilde{P}\tilde{A}-\rho^{2}\tilde{P}&\tilde{A}^{\top}\tilde{P}\tilde{B}\\
\tilde{B}^{\top}\tilde{P}\tilde{A}&\tilde{B}^{\top}\tilde{P}\tilde{B}\end{array}\right)-\tilde{X}\preceq 0,

(MI)

where
\tilde{X}=\tilde{X}_{1}+\rho^{2}\tilde{X}_{2}+(1-\rho^{2})\tilde{X}_{3}
with

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}-L\delta^{2}&L\delta^{2}&-(1-\alpha L)\delta\\
L\delta^{2}&-L\delta^{2}&(1-\alpha L)\delta\\
-(1-\alpha L)\delta&(1-\alpha L)\delta&\alpha(2-\alpha L)\end{bmatrix},\;\;\tilde{X}_{2}:=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}\gamma^{2}\mu&-\gamma^{2}\mu&-\gamma\\
-\gamma^{2}\mu&\gamma^{2}\mu&\gamma\\
-\gamma&\gamma&0\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\quad\tilde{X}_{3}:=\frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}(1+\gamma)^{2}\mu&-\gamma(1+\gamma)\mu&-(1+\gamma)\\
-\gamma(1+\gamma)\mu&\gamma^{2}\mu&\gamma\\
-(1+\gamma)&\gamma&0\end{bmatrix},

and
\delta:=\beta-\gamma
. Then, the Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}_{P}
defined as (
33
) with
P=\tilde{P}\otimes I_{d}
decays with rate
\rho
along the trajectories of
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
,

\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1})\leq\rho^{2}\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}).

(94)

Proof.

The result directly follows from combining
[
20
]
[Lemma 5] and
[
20
]
[Theorem 2].
∎

Lemma B.2
.

The set
\mathcal{S}_{c}
defined in Theorem
2
is not empty. In particular,

\left\{(\vartheta,\psi):\max\Big\{\frac{1}{\kappa}\sqrt{\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}+\kappa}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\leq\vartheta=\psi<1\Big\}\right\}\subset\mathcal{S}_{c}.

Proof.

Assume

\max\{\frac{1}{\kappa},\sqrt{\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}+\kappa}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\}\leq\vartheta=\psi<1
. This would imply
\alpha=\frac{1}{L}
(from the equality (
96
)) and we can write

\displaystyle\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\right]\left[1-\frac{\mu\psi^{2}-L(1-\psi)^{2}}{L\psi}\right]-\left(1-\frac{\psi}{\kappa}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\right]\left[1-\frac{\psi}{\kappa}+\frac{(1-\psi)^{2}}{\psi}-\left(1+\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\right)\left(1-\frac{\psi}{\kappa}\right)\right],

\displaystyle=\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\right]\left[\frac{(1-\psi)^{2}}{\psi}-\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\left(1-\frac{\psi}{\kappa}\right)\right].

From the fact that
\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\geq\frac{\psi}{\kappa}
and
\frac{\psi^{2}}{\kappa^{2}}\leq\frac{\psi^{2}}{\kappa}
, we get

\displaystyle\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\right]\left[1-\frac{\mu\psi^{2}-L(1-\psi)^{2}}{L\psi}\right]-\left(1-\frac{\psi}{\kappa}\right)^{2}\leq\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\psi}{\kappa}}\right]\left[\frac{1-\psi}{\psi}\right]\left(1-\psi-\frac{\psi^{2}}{\kappa}\right).

The condition
\sqrt{\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}+\kappa}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\leq\psi
implies
1-\psi-\frac{\psi^{2}}{\kappa}\leq 0
showing that
(\vartheta,\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
:

\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{(1-\vartheta)\vartheta}{\kappa(1-\psi)}}\right]\left[1-\frac{(1-\vartheta)(\mu\psi^{2}-L(1-\psi)^{2})}{L(1-\psi)\vartheta}\right]\leq\left(1-\frac{(1-\vartheta)\psi}{\kappa(1-\psi)}\right)^{2}.

Moreover, all
\frac{1}{\kappa}\geq\psi
satisfy
\max\{2-\frac{1}{\psi},\frac{1}{1+\kappa(1-\psi)}\}\leq\psi
which proves that
\psi\in\mathcal{S}_{-}
. Hence, we conclude that

\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\vline~\max\left\{\frac{1}{\kappa},\sqrt{\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}+\kappa}-\frac{\kappa}{2}\right\}\leq\vartheta=\psi<1\right\}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\subseteq(\mathcal{S}_{-}\cap\mathcal{S}_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{c}.

∎

B.1
Proof of Theorem
2

The proof is based on providing explicit solutions to (
MI
). Let us first show how (
MI
) can be used to derive an upper bound on the suboptimality,
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
, in Lemma
B.3
.

Lemma B.3
.

Consider the dynamical system (
3
) defined by noisy GMM.
Suppose
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
satisfies (
MI
) for some rate
\rho\in(0,1)
, and a positive semi-definite matrix
P
, then we have the inequality

f(x_{k+1})-f(x_{*})\leq\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1})\leq\rho^{2}\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}^{\top}m_{k}+\alpha^{2}(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P})\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\|^{2},

(95)

where
m_{k}=\alpha\delta L(x_{k-1}-x_{k})-\alpha(1-\alpha L)\nabla f(y_{k})+2B^{\top}P(A({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k}-{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{*})+B\nabla f(y_{k}))
and
\lambda_{P}
is the largest eigenvalue of the
d
-by-
d
principle submatrix of P.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
D.3
.
∎

In order to prove (
36
); we will provide explicit choice of
\rho
and
P
that satisfy (
MI
) and then build on Lemma
B.3
. We consider the left hand-side of (
MI
) and introduce

\textbf{L}=\begin{bmatrix}A^{\top}PA-\rho^{2}P&A^{\top}PB\\
B^{\top}PA&B^{\top}PB\end{bmatrix}-X,

where
A=\tilde{A}\otimes I_{d}
and
B=\tilde{B}\otimes I_{d}
with

\displaystyle\tilde{A}=\begin{bmatrix}(1+\beta)&-\beta\\
1&0\end{bmatrix},\;\;\tilde{B}=\begin{bmatrix}-\alpha\\
0\end{bmatrix}.

By definition of the matrices
A,B,P
and
X
, we can see that
\mathbf{L}=\tilde{L}\otimes I_{d}
and
\mathbf{L}\preceq 0
if and only if
\tilde{L}\preceq 0
. Hence in the rest of the proof, we are going to find the parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
for which
\tilde{L}
satisfies the Sylvester criterion for negative semi-definite matrices. First, we set
\gamma=\psi\beta
and
\tilde{P}=\tilde{p}\tilde{p}^{\top}
where
\tilde{p}^{\top}=[\tilde{p}_{0},-\tilde{p}_{0}+\tilde{p}_{1}]
with
\tilde{p}_{0},\tilde{p}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
that we will specify later. The elements at
i
-th row and
j
-th column (
\tilde{L}_{ij}
) of
\tilde{L}
can be computed as

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{11}

\displaystyle=(\beta^{2}-\rho^{2})\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}+2\beta\tilde{p}_{0}\tilde{p}_{1}+\tilde{p}_{1}^{2}+\frac{L\delta^{2}-\gamma^{2}\mu}{2}-\frac{(1-\rho^{2})(1+2\gamma)\mu}{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{12}

\displaystyle=(\rho^{2}-\beta^{2})\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}-(\beta+\rho^{2})\tilde{p}_{0}\tilde{p}_{1}-\frac{L\delta^{2}-\gamma^{2}\mu}{2}+\frac{(1-\rho^{2})\gamma\mu}{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{22}

\displaystyle=(\beta^{2}-\rho^{2})\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}-\rho^{2}(\tilde{p}_{1}^{2}-2\tilde{p}_{1}\tilde{p}_{0})+\frac{L\delta^{2}-\gamma^{2}\mu}{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{32}

\displaystyle=\alpha\beta\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}-\frac{\beta(1-\alpha L)+\alpha\gamma L}{2},

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{31}

\displaystyle=-\alpha\beta\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}-\alpha\tilde{p}_{1}\tilde{p}_{0}+\frac{\beta(1-\alpha L)+\alpha\gamma L+1-\rho^{2}}{2}=-\tilde{L}_{32}+\frac{1-\rho^{2}}{2}-\alpha\tilde{p}_{1}\tilde{p}_{0},

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{33}

\displaystyle=\alpha^{2}\tilde{p}_{0}^{2}-\frac{\alpha(2-\alpha L)}{2}.

Choosing
\tilde{p}_{1}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2}}
,
\tilde{p}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}}
,
\rho^{2}=1-\sqrt{\alpha\vartheta\mu}
, implies
1-\rho^{2}=2\alpha\tilde{p}_{0}\tilde{p}_{1}
and we get
\tilde{L}_{31}=-\tilde{L}_{32}
. Note that this corresponds to choosing
P
according to (
34
). Under this particular choice of
\tilde{p}_{0},\tilde{p}_{1}
, and
\rho
; if
\alpha,\vartheta
and
\gamma
satisfy the condition

\alpha L(1-\psi)-1+\vartheta=0,

(96)

then
\tilde{L}_{31}=-\tilde{L}_{32}=0
. If we set
\beta=\frac{\rho^{2}}{1-\alpha\psi\mu}\left[1-\sqrt{\frac{\alpha\mu}{\vartheta}}\right]
for
\alpha,\vartheta,\psi>0
satisfying the condition (
96
) and
\rho
as given before; then we can see that
\tilde{L}_{11}=\tilde{L}_{22}=-\tilde{L}_{12}
. Overall, our choice of parameters converts the
matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{L}}
into the following form

\tilde{\textbf{L}}=\begin{bmatrix}-\tilde{L}_{0}&\tilde{L}_{0}&0\\
\tilde{L}_{0}&-\tilde{L}_{0}&0\\
0&0&\tilde{L}_{1}\end{bmatrix},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{0}

\displaystyle=\rho^{2}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2}}\right]^{2}-\beta^{2}\left[\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}+\frac{L(1-\psi)^{2}-\psi^{2}\mu}{2}\right],

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha(1-\psi\alpha L)}{2}.

Notice that (
96
) uniquely determines the value of
\alpha
if
\psi\neq 1
and choosing
\psi=1
directly implies
\vartheta=1
, since
\alpha>0
. In the case of
\psi=1=\vartheta
, we can have any
\alpha\in(0,1/L)
and satisfy (
96
). Therefore, we will study these two cases separately.

The case
\psi\neq 1
:
In this case, the condition (
96
) yields
\alpha=\frac{1-\vartheta}{L(1-\psi)}
. Hence the definitions of
\alpha,\beta,\rho
and
\tilde{L}_{1}
imply that if

(\vartheta,\psi)\in\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{S}:=\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~0<\frac{1-\vartheta}{1-\psi}\leq\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\min\{\kappa\vartheta,\frac{1}{\psi},\frac{\kappa}{\vartheta}\}\;\&\;\psi\neq 1\right\},

then the inequalities

\tilde{L}_{1}<0
,
\beta>0
,
\rho>0
, and
\alpha>0
are satisfied. We can decompose
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{S}
as
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{S}=S_{-}\cup S_{+}
where
S_{-}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{S}\cap\{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~\psi<1\}
and
S_{+}:=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\tilde{S}\cap\{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~\psi>1\}
are given as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~0\leq\psi<1\,\&\,\max\left\{2-\frac{1}{\psi},\frac{1}{1+\kappa(1-\psi)}\right\}\leq\vartheta<1\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{+}

\displaystyle=\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~\psi>1\,\&\,1<\vartheta\leq\min\left\{2-\frac{1}{\psi},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1+4\kappa(\psi-1)}\right\}\right\}.

Next, we will simplify the conditions in
\mathcal{S}_{+}
. Consider the function
p(\psi)=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{4\psi-3}-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{\psi}
for
\psi>1
. Notice that
p(1)=0
and also the derivative
p^{\prime}
satisfies

p^{\prime}(\psi)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\psi-3}}-\frac{1}{\psi^{2}}>0.

for all
\psi>1
. Moreover, for all
\psi>1
we have

\sqrt{1+4(\psi-1)}\leq\sqrt{1+4\kappa(\psi-1)}\implies\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{4\psi-3}-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{1}{\psi}\leq\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{1+4\kappa(\psi-1)}-2+\frac{1}{\psi}.

Hence we obtain,

0<p(\psi)<\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1+4\kappa(\psi-1)}-2+\frac{1}{\psi},

for
\psi>1
and
\kappa>1
. This simplifies the set
\mathcal{S}_{+}
to the following

\mathcal{S}_{+}=\left\{(\vartheta,\psi)~\mid~\psi>1\,\&\,1<\vartheta\leq 2-\frac{1}{\psi}\right\}.

The inequality
\tilde{L}_{0}\geq 0
, on the other hand, holds if and only if

\beta^{2}\left[\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}+\frac{L(1-\psi)^{2}-\psi^{2}\mu}{2}\right]\leq\rho^{2}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}}-\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{2}}\right]^{2}
, which is true if and only if

\rho^{2}\left[1-\alpha\frac{\mu\psi^{2}-L(1-\psi)^{2}}{\vartheta}\right]\leq(1-\alpha\psi\mu)^{2}.

(97)

Notice that above inequality equals to the inequality that defines the set
\mathcal{S}_{1}
by the definitions of the parameters
\rho^{2}
and
\alpha
. Hence we can see that for
(\vartheta,\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{1}
, we have
\tilde{L}_{0}\geq 0
Therefore the parameters
(\vartheta,\psi)
belonging to set
(\mathcal{S}_{-}\cup\mathcal{S}_{+})\cap\mathcal{S}_{1}

implies that the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{L}}
is negative semi-definite from the Sylvester theorem.

The case
\psi=1
.
The condition (
96
) implies that
\vartheta=1
, as well. Hence we would obtain

\displaystyle\rho^{2}=1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu},\;\beta=\frac{1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu}}{1+\sqrt{\alpha\mu}}=\gamma.

This satisfies
\rho>0
,
\beta>0
,
\tilde{L}_{1}\leq 0
, and

\tilde{L}_{0}=\frac{(1-\alpha\mu)\sqrt{\alpha\mu}(1-\sqrt{\alpha\mu})^{2}}{2\alpha(1+\sqrt{\alpha\mu})}>0,

for
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{L}
. Therefore, if
(\vartheta,\psi)\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
, (
MI
) is satisfied with the parameters
(\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi},\beta_{\vartheta,\psi},\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi})
and rate
\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}
.

The Sylvester theorem implies that the matrix
L
with given choice of parameters
(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)
, rate
\rho
, and the matrix
P
satisfies (
MI
). Hence the assumptions of Lemma
B.3
are satisfied and we can write the inequality (
95
) for the iterates
x_{k}
generated by noisy GMM with parameters
(\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi},\beta_{\vartheta,\psi},\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi})
:

f(x_{k+1})-f(x_{*})\leq\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1})\leq\rho^{2}\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}^{\top}m_{k}+\frac{\alpha(L\alpha+\vartheta)}{2}\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\|^{2},

where we used
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}
.

If we take the conditional expectation of the above inequality with respect to the natural filtration
\mathcal{F}_{k}
generated by the iterates
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}
, use the tower property of the conditional expectation with the facts that
\mathbb{E}[\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]\leq\sigma^{2}d
and
\mathbb{E}[w_{k+1}|\mathcal{F}_{k}]=0
(from Assumption
1
), and recurse over
k
, we obtain the desired result.

B.2
Proof of Proposition
3

We are going to prove Proposition
3
by providing an upper bound on the finite-horizon risk measure,
\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)
. Our proof strategy is to use the contraction property of the Lyapunov function
\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})
and the fact that
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\leq\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})
.
For simplicity, we drop the subscript
P
in
\mathcal{V}_{P}
and instead adopt
\mathcal{V}
, if it is clear from the context. Similarly, we set
\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}\rightarrow\alpha
,
\beta_{\vartheta,\psi}\rightarrow\beta
,
\gamma_{\vartheta,\psi}\rightarrow\gamma
,
\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi}\rightarrow\mathtt{v}
,
\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}\rightarrow\bar{\rho}
, and
\rho_{\vartheta,\psi}\rightarrow\rho
. Recall that we have shown in the proof of Theorem
2
that this choice of parameters together with the matrix
P
given in Theorem
2
satisfy (
MI
) and the largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{P}
of the
d
-by-
d
principle submatrix of
P
is
\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}
, that is
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}
. This also implies the following inequality (see the inequality (
127
) from the Proof of Lemma
B.3
),

\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1})\leq\rho^{2}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})+m_{k}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}+\alpha^{2}(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P})\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\|^{2},

(98)

where
\lambda_{P}
is the largest eigenvalue of
d
-by-
d
principle submatrix of
P
and
m_{k}
is as given in Lemma
B.3
. We next show that the inequality (
98
), in fact, provides an upper bound on the finite-horizon entropic risk. By Assumption
1
, the noise
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}=\tilde{\nabla}f(y_{k})-\nabla f(y_{k})
is independent from the sigma-algebra,
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, generated by
\{x_{j}\}_{j=0}^{k}
; therefore, by the tower property of conditional expectations and the inequality (
98
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k+1})}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(\rho^{2}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})+m_{k}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}+\alpha^{2}(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P})\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\|^{2}\right)}\right],

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\rho^{2}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(m_{k}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}+\alpha^{2}(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P})\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\|^{2}\right)}~\vline~\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]\right].

(99)

Since the noise is Gaussian, we can explicitly compute the conditional expectation at the right-hand side of the equation (
99
) as follows.

Lemma B.4
.

Let
\alpha,\lambda_{P},\theta,L
be non-negative constants and
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a Gaussian random vector satisfying Assumption
1
. Then, the inequality

\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}m}^{\top}w+\alpha^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\lambda_{P}+\frac{L}{2})}\|w\|^{2})}]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\frac{1}{(1-\theta\alpha^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(\lambda_{P}+L/2)})^{d/2}}e^{\frac{\theta^{2}\|m\|^{2}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}4}\sigma^{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}-\theta\alpha^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(2\lambda_{P}+L)})}},

(100)

holds for any vector
m\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\theta
satisfying for all
\theta\alpha^{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}(2\lambda_{P}+L)}<2
.

Proof.

The proof is provided in
D.4
.
∎

It is easy to check that
\theta_{u}^{g}=\frac{2(1-\rho^{2})}{8\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}+(1-\rho^{2})\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}
, since
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}
, and by (
37
),

\theta<\frac{2(1-\rho^{2})}{8\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}+(1-\rho^{2})\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}<\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}.

(101)

Hence, the assumptions of Lemma
B.4
hold under the setting of Proposition
3
and setting
m=m_{k}
and
\lambda_{P}
as the largest eigenvalue of the

-by-
d
principle submatrix of
P
in Lemma
B.4
gives the conditional expectation (
99
) as

\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\left(m_{k}^{\top}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}+\alpha^{2}(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P})\|{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}w_{k+1}}\|^{2}\right)}~\vline~\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\Big(1-\theta\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)\Big)^{-d/2}e^{\frac{\theta^{2}\|m_{k}\|^{2}}{4\sigma^{2}(2-\theta\alpha^{2}(2\lambda_{P}+L))}}.

(102)

Next, we present an upper bound on the norm of the vector
m_{k}
in Lemma
B.5
.

Lemma B.5
.

The vector
m_{k}
given in Lemma
B.3
under the premise of Proposition
3
admits the following inequality

\|m_{k}\|^{2}\leq 8\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}\left(\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})+\mathcal{V}_{P}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k-1})\right),

(103)

where
\mathtt{v}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}=\frac{2L^{2}}{\mu}(2\delta^{2}+(1-\alpha L)^{2}(1+2\gamma+2\gamma^{2}))+\lambda_{P}\rho^{2}
,
\rho
and
P
are as in Lemma
B.3
and
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}
is the largest eigenvalue of
d
-by-
d
principle submatrix of
P
.

Proof.

The proof is provided in Appendix
D.5
.
∎

Notice that
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\leq\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})
; therefore, we can write

\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))}]\leq\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})}].

(104)

It follows from (
98
), (
99
), (
102
), (
103
), and (
104
) that

\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))}]\\
\leq\big(1-\theta\alpha^{2}(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P})\big)^{-d/2}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\big(\rho^{2}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k-1})+\frac{4\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\theta}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(2\lambda_{P}+L)}(\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k-1})+\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k-2}))\big)}\right],

(105)

which also provides an upper bound on the finite horizon entropic risk measure after taking logarithms.
However, the inequality (
105
) is not very useful due to its dependency on the iterates
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k-1}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k-2}
. Therefore, in the following Lemma
B.6
, we further analyze (
105
) and derive an upper bound on the moment generating function of
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
which depends only on the initialization
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}
.

Lemma B.6
.

Consider the setting of Proposition
3
where
\theta<\theta_{u}^{g}
. Consider the iterates
z_{k}=\begin{bmatrix}x_{k}^{\top},x_{k-1}^{\top}\end{bmatrix}^{\top}
of the noisy GMM. We have

\mathbb{E}[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}(f(x_{k})-f(x_{*}))}]\leq\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}\big(1-\theta\bar{\rho}^{2j}_{\vartheta,\psi}\alpha^{2}_{\vartheta,\psi}(\lambda_{P}+\frac{L}{2})\big)^{-d/2}e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\bar{\rho}^{2k}_{\vartheta,\psi}2\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0})},

(106)

where
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi}}
.

Proof.

The proof is deferred to Appendix
D.6
.
∎

We are now ready to derive a bound on the finite-horizon entropic risk measure using Lemma
B.6
:

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)\leq\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\theta}{2\sigma^{2}}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{k})}\right]\leq\bar{\rho}^{2k}2\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0})-\frac{\sigma^{2}d}{\theta}\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\log\Big(1-\theta\bar{\rho}^{2j}\alpha^{2}\big(\lambda_{P}+\frac{L}{2}\big)\Big).

The fact that
-\log(x)\leq 1-\frac{1}{x}
for all
x\in[0,1]
implies

-\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log\Big(1-\theta\bar{\rho}^{2j}\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)\Big)\leq\frac{\sigma^{2}d\bar{\rho}^{2j}\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)}{1-\theta\bar{\rho}^{2j}\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)}<\frac{\sigma^{2}d\bar{\rho}^{2j}\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)}{1-\theta\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)},

where the last inequality follows from
\bar{\rho}<1
(see also the derivation of (
134
)). Therefore, we get

\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)<\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)}{1-\theta\alpha^{2}\big(\frac{L}{2}+\lambda_{P}\big)}\right)\frac{1-\bar{\rho}^{2k}}{1-\bar{\rho}^{2}}+\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2\bar{\rho}^{2k}\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}).

Then the inequality (
38
) follows from the fact that
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}
and the definitions of the rest of parameters as given in Theorem
2
.

B.3
Proof of Theorem
3

The proof builds upon the results of Proposition
3
. During the proof, to simplify the notation, we will drop the subscripts in
\alpha_{\vartheta,\psi},\rho_{\vartheta,\psi},\mathtt{v}_{\vartheta,\psi},\bar{\rho}_{\vartheta,\psi}
and
\bar{\bar{\rho}}^{(\varphi)}_{\vartheta,\psi}
.
We will also drop the subscript
P
in
\mathcal{V}_{P}
for simplicity. Recall that
\lambda_{P}
is the largest eigenvalue of
d
-by-
d
principle submatrix of
P
given in Theorem
2
and is given as
\lambda_{P}=\frac{\vartheta}{2\alpha}
. The inequality (
38
) implies that the EV@R of
f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})
at each iteration
k
satisfies the inequality,

\displaystyle EV@R_{1-\zeta}\left[f(x_{k})-f(x_{*})\right]

\displaystyle=\inf_{0<\theta}\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(1/\zeta),

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{0<\theta<\theta_{u}^{g}}\mathbf{r}_{k,\sigma^{2}}(\theta)+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(1/\zeta),

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\leq}\inf_{0<\theta<\theta_{u}^{g}}\frac{\sigma^{2}d\alpha\big(\vartheta+\alpha L\big)}{(1-\bar{\rho}^{2})(2-\theta\alpha\big(\vartheta+\alpha L\big))}+\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{\theta}\log(1/\zeta)+\bar{\rho}^{2k}2\mathcal{V}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}z}_{0}).

(107)

Notice that
\bar{\rho}
also depends on
\theta
, in particular, if
\theta=\theta_{u}^{g}
then we would have

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{1-\rho^{2}}{8\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}+(1-\rho^{2})\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}

\displaystyle\;\iff 8\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}\theta=2(1-\rho^{2})-\theta(1-\rho^{2})\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P}),

\displaystyle\;\iff\frac{\theta 16\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}=4(1-\rho^{2})-\frac{\theta 16\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})},

\displaystyle\;\iff\left(\rho^{2}+\frac{\theta 4\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}\right)^{2}+\frac{\theta 16\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}=\left(2-\rho^{2}-\frac{\theta 4\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\;\iff\sqrt{\left(\rho^{2}+\frac{\theta 4\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}\right)^{2}+\frac{\theta 16\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})}}=2-\rho^{2}-\frac{\theta 4\alpha^{2}\mathtt{v}}{2-\theta\alpha^{2}(L+2\lambda_{P})},

which implies that
\bar{\rho}^{2}=1
. Therefore, we require the strict inequality
\theta<\theta_{u}^{g}
so that right-hand side of (
107
) stays finite. On the other hand,
\bar{\rho}
depends on
\theta
and this makes solving the minimization problem in (
107
) explicitly non-trivial. As we shall discuss next, we can overcome this issue by shrinking the constraint from
(0,\theta_{u}^{g})
to
(0,\theta_{\varphi}^{g}]
where
\theta_{\varphi}^{g}=\varphi\theta_{u}^{g}
for a fixed
\varphi\in(0,1)
. Recall the notations
